The nerve theorem for relative monads
Nathanael Arkor, Dylan McDermott

Abstract
. A fundamental result in the theory of monads is the characterisation of the category of algebras for a monad in terms of a pullback of the category of presheaves on the category of free algebras: intuitively, this expresses that every algebra is a colimit of free algebras. We establish an analogous result for enriched relative monads with dense roots, and explain how it generalises the nerve theorems for monads with arities and nervous monads. As an application, we derive sufficient conditions for the existence of algebraic colimits of relative monads. More generally, we establish such a characterisation of the category of algebras in the context of an exact virtual equipment. In doing so, we are led to study the relationship between a j j -relative monad T T and its associated loose-monad E ⁡ ( j , T ) E(j,T) , and consequently show that the opalgebra object and the algebra object for T T may be constructed from certain double categorical limits and colimits associated to E ⁡ ( j , T ) E(j,T) .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
18D70,18D65,18C15,18C20,18A40,18D60,18C10,18D20,18N10

1.
Introduction

A monad may be viewed as an abstraction of the specification of an algebraic structure by operators and equations
[
Law63
,
Lin66
,
Lin66a
]
. Consequently, an algebra for a monad is an abstraction of the classical notion of an algebra qua a set equipped with algebraic structure. Classically, every algebra may be presented as a quotient of a free algebra. The same is true for algebras for monads: given a monad
T=(t,\mu,\eta)
and a
T
-algebra
(a,\alpha)
, the following diagram forms a coequaliser in the category of
T
-algebras
[
Bec66
]
.

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{a}}

Consequently, every
T
-algebra is a colimit of free
T
-algebras. This observation may be refined by considering not just an individual
T
-algebra, but the entire category of
T
-algebras. Denote by
u_{T}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to A
the forgetful functor from the category of algebras; by
k_{T}\colon A\to\mathbf{Kl}(T)
the inclusion functor into the category of free algebras (i.e. the Kleisli category); and by
{\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}\colon A\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
the Yoneda embedding. Then the following diagram forms a pullback of categories
[
Lin69
, Observation 1.1]
.

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Assuming that
A
is small, the presheaf category
[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is the free cocompletion of the category of free
T
-algebras under all small colimits. The pullback above exhibits the category of algebras as a full subcategory of the presheaf category, which is furthermore closed under representables, since every free algebra is, in particular, an algebra.
Consequently, the category of algebras may be seen as a cocompletion of the category of free algebras under a class of small colimits
[
Kel82
, Theorem 5.19]
.

The primary purpose of this paper is to generalise this result to
relative monads
, which generalise monads by permitting the underlying functors to be arbitrary functors, rather than endofunctors
[
ACU10
,
ACU15
]
. Specifically, given a dense functor
j\colon A\to E
, we prove that, for a
j
-relative monad
T
, the following diagram forms a pullback of categories, where we denote by
n_{j}\colon E\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
the nerve of
j
, which sends an object
e\in|E|
to the restricted Yoneda embedding
a\mapsto E(ja,e)
.

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Furthermore, the unlabelled functor above is isomorphic to the nerve of the comparison functor
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Kl}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
and consequently exhibits the
T
-algebras as certain presheaves on the category of free
T
-algebras: namely, those presheaves in the essential image of the nerve
n_{i_{T}}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. We therefore call this the
nerve theorem
for relative monads. Such a theorem is of interest for several reasons. From a purely conceptual point of view, it establishes that (under the mild assumption of density of the root
j
) algebras for relative monads are colimits of free algebras. This is deeper an observation than might appear at first, since algebras for relative monads do not admit a coequaliser presentation
1
1

1

A similar obstruction arises when one attempts to generalise the classical monadicity theorem
[
Bec66
]
, which is formulated in terms of coequalisers, to relative monads. However, there, too, it is possible to entirely avoid consideration of coequalisers
[
AM25
]
(cf.
[
Par71
]
).
like that for monads (
1
), as it is not possible to iterate the underlying functor
t\colon A\to E
.
From a practical point of view, it facilitates simple proofs that the category of algebras for a (relative) monad inherits structure from its base: for instance, it follows easily from (
3
) that the category of algebras for an accessible monad on a locally presentable category is itself locally presentable. But perhaps of most interest is the role of the theorem in categorical logic.

Indeed, despite relative monads and their algebras having been introduced only in the last 15 years, pullbacks resembling (
3
) are much older. A similar pullback first appeared in
[
Lin69
, §2]
, in
[
Lin69
]
’s study of
[
Law63
]
’s structure–semantics adjunction, which relates algebraic theories to their categories of algebras
[
Law63
, Theorem III.1.2]
. Motivated by
[
Lin69
]
, such pullbacks appeared also in the work of
[
Die74
]

[
Die74
,
Die76
]
and of
[
Lee77
, Theorem 2.2.7]
, both of whom took (
3
) as the
definition
of categories of algebras for structures that turn out to be equivalent to relative monads
[
AM24
]
. More recently, the pullback (
3
) has been rediscovered in work on generalisations of the notion of algebraic theory, and nerve theorems for associated classes of monads
[
Lei04a
,
Web07
,
NP09
,
LP09
,
Mel10
,
LR11
,
BMW12
,
BG19
,
LP23
]
. However, the connection to relative monads has not been observed
2
2

2

Though see
Remark

1.2
.
.
In fact, the nerve theorem for relative monads strictly subsumes the nerve theorems for monads that have appeared previously in the literature. Thus, the importance of the nerve theorem for relative monads has essentially already been appreciated throughout the categorical literature, albeit implicitly.

1.1.
Relative monads and distributors

While it is entirely possible simply to give a direct proof of the nerve theorem (as we shall do in
Theorem

2.6
), this sheds little light on the conceptual reason the theorem holds. Thus, the secondary purpose of this paper is to elucidate the phenomenon.

The key to understanding the nature of (
3
) lies in the study of the connection between the theory of relative monads and the theory of distributors (a.k.a. profunctors or (bi)modules). It was observed in
[
AM24
]
that to every
(j\colon A\to E)
-relative monad
T
there is an associated
loose-monad
(a.k.a. promonad, profunctor monad, or arrow)
E(j,T)
on
A
(cf.
[
Die75
]
). In fact, the connection between relative monads and loose-monads is very strong:
j
-relative monads can be characterised as loose-monads whose carrier has the form
E(j,t)
, for a functor
t\colon A\to E
, satisfying a natural compatibility condition (
Proposition

4.9
). In other words, the
j
-relative monad
T
and the loose-monad
E(j,T)
may be seen as different presentations of the same structure. It is then natural to ask to what extent we can understand relative monads via their associated loose-monads: for instance, whether we can capture the
T
-algebras (a.k.a. the left-modules, which are classified by the Eilenberg–Moore category) and
T
-opalgebras (a.k.a. the right-modules, which are classified by the Kleisli category) via natural structures associated to
E(j,T)
.

This turns out to be the case:
T
-algebras may be characterised as certain loose-monad modules into
E(j,T)
, assuming
j
is dense; while
T
-opalgebras may be characterised as certain loose-monad morphisms from
E(j,T)
. Since the Kleisli category and the Eilenberg–Moore category for
T
exhibit a universal opalgebra and a universal algebra for
T
respectively, we may thus characterise the Kleisli category as a certain universal loose-monad morphism associated to
E(j,T)
– its
collapse

[
Sch15
,
Woo85
]
– and the Eilenberg–Moore category as a certain universal loose-monad module associated to
E(j,T)
– its
semanticiser
(
Definition

6.4
). The collapse of a loose-monad is a kind of (virtual) double categorical colimit, while the semanticiser is a kind of (virtual) double categorical limit. The final step in understanding the nerve theorem is to observe that semanticisers may be constructed from pullbacks and presheaf categories, which, in particular, recovers the nerve theorem (
3
).

In the special case of non-relative monads, the relationship between monads and loose-monads, and the connection to the theory of algebras and opalgebras, was first observed by
[
Woo85
]
in the setting of proarrow equipments, inspired by
[
Thi71
]
’s study of the structure–semantics adjunction
[
Thi71
]
. However, it is fair to say that this relationship is not well known, and the perspective we present is new. Therefore, we hope that this paper serves as an exposition of these ideas even in the non-relative case.

Before we begin, we should say a word about the setting in which we work. While we have spoken so far only about unenriched relative monads, the results we have mentioned hold more generally. Following
[
AM24
,
AM25
]
, we shall work throughout in the context of a
virtual equipment

[
CS10
]
, which is a two-dimensional framework for formal category theory. This permits us to establish our theorems for various flavours of category theory at once: in particular, we obtain a nerve theorem for enriched relative monads (
Theorem

8.8
) by instantiating the main theorem (
Theorem

7.14
) in the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
of categories enriched in a monoidal category
{\mathbb{V}}
.

1.2.
Outline of the paper

We begin in
Section

2
by sketching a direct proof of the nerve theorem for unenriched relative monads, presenting several applications of the theorem, and discussing the connection to nerve theorems for monads. In
Section

3
, we briefly recall the formal categorical concepts from
[
AM24
]
that will be fundamental to our study of the nerve theorem. In
Section

4
, we study the canonical loose-monad associated to a relative monad, and thereby characterise relative monads as certain sections of loose-monads. We take a brief interlude in
Section

5
to introduce exactness for virtual equipments, which is necessary to relate the algebra objects and opalgebra objects for a relative monad
T
to its associated loose-monad
E(j,T)
, which we do in
Section

6
. In
Section

7
, we show that, assuming the existence of presheaf objects, we obtain a formal nerve theorem; and in
Section

8
demonstrate how the formal theory may be applied to
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
to obtain a nerve theorem for enriched relative monads. We conclude in
Section

9
by observing that loose-monads may be seen as monads relative to Yoneda embeddings (cf.
[
ACU15
, §5]
). Finally, in
Appendix

A
we include a strictification result for virtual equipments (
Theorem

A.1
) and pseudo equipments (
Corollary

A.3
), which provides conceptual justification for a simplifying assumption taken in the paper (
Definition

5.1
).

Remark 1.1
.

In
[
Ark22
, §5.5]
, the first-named author gave an alternative proof of the nerve theorem for relative monads, based on embeddings of relative monads rather than on distributors. We shall relate the two approaches in future work.

Remark 1.2
.

After presenting an early version of this work to the Masaryk University Algebra Seminar, the authors were informed that John Bourke, Marcelo Fiore, and Richard Garner have, in unpublished joint work, independently established a nerve theorem for enriched relative monads.

1.3.
Notation

We have chosen to refrain from some common abuses of notation. We denote the pullback of a cospan
f\colon A\to C\leftarrow B\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mug
by
f\times_{C}g
rather than by
A\times_{C}B
. We denote the underlying function of an (enriched) functor
f\colon A\to B
by
|f|\colon|A|\to|B|
rather than by
f\colon|A|\to|B|
. For morphisms
f\colon a\to b
and
g\colon b\to c
, we denote by
(f\mathbin{;}g)\colon a\to c
or
gf\colon a\to c
their composite.

Proposition

4.7
; and thank John Bourke for insights about limits of locally presentable categories.

2.
The nerve theorem and its applications

We begin by presenting the nerve theorem for unenriched relative monads (
Theorem

2.6
), sketching a direct proof of the theorem, and giving several applications. The remainder of the paper is devoted to giving an abstract justification for the theorem (
Sections

4
,
5
,
6
and
7
) and thereby deducing the nerve theorem for enriched relative monads (
Section

8
). For convenience, we first recall the definitions central to this section.

2.1.
Relative adjunctions and relative monads

Definition 2.1

(
[
Ulm68
, Definition 2.2]
)
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a functor. A
j
-relative adjunction
(or simply
j
-adjunction
) in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
comprises

(2.1.(1))

a functor
\ell\colon A\to C
;

(2.1.(2))

a functor
r\colon C\to E
;

(2.1.(3))

an isomorphism
C(|\ell|x,y)\cong E(|j|x,|r|y)
natural in
x\in|A|
and
y\in|C|
.

We denote this situation by
\ell\mathrel{\dashv_{j}}r
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Just as every adjunction induces a monad, every
j
-adjunction induces a
j
-monad.

Definition 2.2

(
[
ACU15
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a functor. A
j
-relative monad
(or simply
j
-monad
) in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
comprises

(2.2.(1))

for each object
x\in|A|
, an object
|t|x\in|E|
;

(2.2.(2))

for each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
in
E
, a morphism
f^{\dagger}\colon|t|x\to|t|y
in
E
;

(2.2.(3))

for each object
x\in|A|
, a morphism
\eta_{x}\colon|j|x\to|t|x
in
E
,

satisfying the following laws.

(2.2.(4))

\eta_{x}\mathbin{;}f^{\dagger}=f
for each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
in
E
.

(2.2.(5))

{\eta_{x}}^{\dagger}=1_{|t|x}
for each object
x\in|A|
.

(2.2.(6))

(f\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger}
for each pair of morphisms
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
and
g\colon|j|y\to|t|z
in
E
.

It follows that
|t|
extends uniquely to a functor
t\colon A\to E
for which
{\dagger}
and
\eta
are natural
[
AM24
, Theorem 8.12]
.

Definition 2.3

(
[
ACU15
, §2.3]
)
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a functor and let
T=(t,{\dagger},\eta)
be a
j
-monad. The
Kleisli category

\mathbf{Kl}(T)
has the same objects as
A
, and has morphisms given by
\mathbf{Kl}(T)(x,y)\mathrel{:=}E(|j|x,|t|y)
. The identity morphism for an object
x\in|A|
is given by
\eta_{x}\colon|j|x\to|t|x
in
E
; and the composite of morphisms
f\colon x\to y
and
g\colon y\to z
in
\mathbf{Kl}(T)
is given by
(f\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger})\colon|j|x\to|t|z
in
E
.

The Kleisli category for a
j
-monad forms a
j
-adjunction, whose left adjoint sends a morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
A
to
(f\mathbin{;}\eta_{y})\colon|j|x\to|t|y
in
E
, and whose right adjoint is given by the action of
{\dagger}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Kl}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Definition 2.4

(
[
ACU15
, Definition 2.11]
)
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a functor and let
T=(t,{\dagger},\eta)
be a
j
-monad. A
T
-algebra
comprises

(2.4.(1))

an object
e\in|E|
;

(2.4.(2))

for each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to e
in
E
, a morphism
f^{\rtimes}\colon|t|x\to e
in
E
,

satisfying the following laws.

(2.4.(3))

\eta_{x}\mathbin{;}f^{\dagger}=f
for each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to e
in
E
.

(2.4.(4))

(f\mathbin{;}g^{\rtimes})^{\rtimes}=f^{\dagger}\mathbin{;}g^{\rtimes}
for each pair of morphisms
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
and
g\colon|j|y\to e
in
E
.

A
T
-algebra morphism
from
(e,{\rtimes})
to
(e^{\prime},{\rtimes}^{\prime})
is a morphism
\epsilon\colon e\to e^{\prime}
such that
f^{\rtimes}\mathbin{;}\epsilon=(f\mathbin{;}\epsilon)^{{\rtimes}^{\prime}}
for each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to e
in
E
.
T
-algebras and their morphisms form a category
\mathbf{Alg}(T)
.

It follows that, for each
T
-algebra
(e,{\rtimes})
, the extension operator
{\rtimes}
is natural. The category of algebras for a
j
-monad forms a
j
-adjunction, whose left adjoint is given by applying
t
, and whose right adjoint forgets the
T
-algebra structure.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

There is a canonical fully faithful comparison functor
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Kl}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
, which sends each object
x\in|A|
to
|t|x
, and each morphism
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
in
E
to
f^{\dagger}
, thus exhibiting
\mathbf{Kl}(T)
as the category of free
T
-algebras.

2.2.
The nerve theorem

Definition 2.5
.

For a functor
j\colon A\to E
between locally small categories, we denote by
n_{j}\colon E\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
the
nerve
of
j
, which sends an object
e\in|E|
to the restricted Yoneda embedding
a\mapsto E(ja,e)
. By definition,
j
is
dense
if its nerve
n_{j}
is fully faithful.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense functor between locally small categories. For a
j
-monad
T
, the category of
T
-algebras exhibits the following pullback of categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Furthermore, the unlabelled functor above is isomorphic to the nerve of the comparison functor
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Kl}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
, exhibiting
i_{T}
as a dense functor.

Proof (sketch).

Given a
T
-algebra
(e\in|E|,{\rtimes}\colon E(j{-},e)\Rightarrow E(t{-},e))
, we define a presheaf on
\mathbf{Kl}(T)
by the following assignments,

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto E(|j|a,e)

\displaystyle(f\colon|j|a\to|t|a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big((g\colon|j|a^{\prime}\to e)\mapsto((f\mathbin{;}g^{\rtimes})\colon|j|a\to e)\big)

with functoriality following from the two
T
-algebra laws. A
T
-algebra morphism induces a natural transformation between the corresponding presheaves by postcomposition. This defines a functor
\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
rendering the square above commutative, and thus induces a functor
\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to n_{j}\times_{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
into the pullback.

In the other direction, given an object
e\in|E|
and a presheaf
p\colon\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
in the pullback, observe that each Kleisli morphism
f\colon|j|a\to|t|a^{\prime}
induces a function
p(f)\colon p(a^{\prime})=E(|j|a^{\prime},e)\to E(|j|a,e)=p(a)
. This assignment defines a natural transformation
E(|j|a,|t|a^{\prime})\times E(|j|a^{\prime},e)\to E(|j|a,e)
, which, by transposing, is equivalently a natural transformation
E(|j|a^{\prime},e)\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}](E(j{-},|t|a^{\prime}),E(j{-},e))
. When
j
is dense, so that
n_{j}
is fully faithful, the codomain is isomorphic to
E(|t|a^{\prime},e)
, which produces a function
E(|j|a^{\prime},e)\to E(|t|a^{\prime},e)
for each
a^{\prime}\in|A|
. This family of functions forms a
T
-algebra structure on
e
, the
T
-algebra laws following from functoriality of
p
. Furthermore, given a morphism
\epsilon\colon e\to e^{\prime}
and a natural transformation
p\Rightarrow p^{\prime}
in the pullback, naturality of the latter implies that
\epsilon
is a morphism between the induced
T
-algebras. The assignment thus extends to a functor
n_{j}\times_{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
.

We leave to the reader the routine checks that the functors
\mathbf{Alg}(T)\rightleftarrows n_{j}\times_{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
thus defined are inverse to one another.

Finally, since
f_{T}\mathrel{\dashv_{j}}u_{T}
, and
f_{T}=(k_{T}\mathbin{;}i_{T})
, we have an isomorphism

E(|j|a,e)\cong\mathbf{Alg}(T)(|i_{T}k_{T}|a,(e,{\rtimes}))=\mathbf{Alg}(T)(|i_{T}|a,(e,{\rtimes}))

natural in
(e,{\rtimes})\in|\mathbf{Alg}(T)|
, using that
k_{T}
is identity-on-objects. Thus, to establish that the unlabelled functor is isomorphic to the nerve of
i_{T}
, it is enough to verify that this isomorphism is natural in
a\in|\mathbf{Kl}(T)|
. However, naturality corresponds to the commutativity of the following square, for each morphism
f\colon|j|a\to|t|a^{\prime}
, which is trivial.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(|j|a,e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(|j|a^{\prime},e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)(|i_{T}|a,(e,{\rtimes}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)(|i_{T}|a^{\prime},(e,{\rtimes}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\rtimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{;}{({-})}^{\rtimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\rtimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\mathbin{;}{({-})})^{\rtimes}}

Thus the unlabelled functor is isomorphic to the nerve
n_{i_{T}}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. That the nerve is fully faithful (and hence that
i_{T}
is dense) follows from stability of fully faithful functors under pullback.
∎

Dually, the category of coalgebras for a relative comonad may be expressed as a pullback over a category of copresheaves. (Since the theory of relative comonads is formally dual to the theory of relative monads, we shall leave subsequent dualisations for the reader to spell out.)

Theorem
2.6
co
.

Let
i\colon Z\to U
be a codense functor. For an
i
-comonad
D
, the category of
D
-coalgebras exhibits the following pullback of categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Coalg}(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(D),{\mathbf{Set}}]{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Z,{\mathbf{Set}}]{}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{D},{\mathbf{Set}}]{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Above, we denote by
m_{i}\colon U\to[Z,{\mathbf{Set}}]{}^{\mathrm{op}}
the co-nerve of
i
, which sends an object
u\in|U|
to the restricted Yoneda embedding
z\mapsto U(u,iz)
. Furthermore, the unlabelled functor above is isomorphic to the co-nerve of the comparison functor
i_{D}\colon\mathbf{Kl}(D)\to\mathbf{Coalg}(D)
, exhibiting
i_{D}
as a codense functor.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

When
j
is not dense, there is still a canonical comparison functor

\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to n_{j}\times_{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}[{k_{T}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]

from the category of algebras into the pullback, but it is not, in general, an equivalence. For instance, denote by
V
the freestanding span
\{\triangledown\leftarrow\lozenge\to\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\triangledown$}}\}
and let
j\colon 1\to V
denote the constant functor on
\lozenge
.
j
is not dense, since
V(\lozenge,\triangledown)\cong 1\cong V(\lozenge,\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\triangledown$}})
but
V(\triangledown,\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\triangledown$}})=\emptyset
. Consider
T
the constant
j
-monad on

\triangledown

.
k_{T}
is the identity on
1
, so that
{k_{T}}^{*}
is the identity on
[1{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\simeq{\mathbf{Set}}
, and thus the apex of the pullback is
V
. However,
\mathbf{Alg}(T)\cong 1\not\simeq V
, since there is a unique
T
-algebra (whose underlying object is

\triangledown

).

On the other hand, there are cases in which
j
is not dense, but the square (
3
) in
Theorem

2.6
is still a pullback. For instance, suppose that
j\colon A\to E
is fully faithful. Then the Kleisli category for
j
, viewed as a trivial
j
-monad, is isomorphic to
A

[
AM24
, Proposition 6.55]
, while the category of algebras is
E

[
AM24
, Proposition 6.42]
. Therefore, the square (
3
) trivially forms a pullback in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
, as shown below. However,
j
need not be dense.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(j){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(For an alternative proof that the nerve theorem holds when
j=t
and
j
is fully faithful, observe that, in the proof of
Theorem

2.6
, we then have that

[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}](E(j{-},|j|a^{\prime}),E(j{-},e))\cong[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}](A({-},a^{\prime}),E(j{-},e))\cong E(|j|a^{\prime},e)

using full faithfulness of
j
, followed by the Yoneda lemma.)

Remark 2.8
.

Pullbacks of identity-on-objects functors along nerves, as below, were first considered by
[
Lin69
, §5]
, who called objects of the pullback
k
-algebras (rel.
j
)
. Indeed, elements of the proof of
Theorem

2.6
are present loc. cit. However,
[
Lin69
]
had no notion of relative monad, nor of algebras thereof, to formulate the nerve theorem at this level of generality.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[K{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Later,
[
Die75
]
and
[
Lee77
]
independently established the equivalence of the following structures
[
Die75
,
Lee77
]
.

(2.8.(1))

Pullbacks of the form in
Theorem

2.6
.

(2.8.(2))

Terminal resolutions of
j
-monads.

(2.8.(3))

Right
j
-adjoints creating
j
-absolute colimits.

However, neither author had an independent definition of the category of algebras for a relative monad:
[
Die75
]
took (1) to be the definition thereof, whilst
[
Lee77
]
took (2) to be the definition. Therefore,
Theorem

2.6
, which adds a fourth equivalent characterisation to the list above, is entirely new.

(2.8.(4))

Categories of algebras for
j
-monads.

(In our formal setting, the equivalence between (2 & 4) follows from the universal property of algebra objects
[
AM24
, Corollary 6.41]
, while the equivalence between (3 & 4) follows from the relative monadicity theorem
[
AM25
, Corollary 4.8]
.)

The proof of
Theorem

2.6
readily generalises, with appropriate modifications, to enriched relative monads. However, rather than give such a concrete proof, we shall derive the enriched nerve theorem (
Theorem

8.8
) from a more general result using formal techniques.

2.3.
Applications

We give a number of applications of the nerve theorem. In our examples, we shall freely make use of the enriched nerve theorem, which will be proven in
Theorem

8.8
. For simplicity, we shall here take
{\mathbb{V}}
to be a closed monoidal locally presentable category, while noting that the nerve theorem itself holds under much weaker assumptions on the base of enrichment
{\mathbb{V}}
.

First, we observe that the nerve theorem for relative monads does indeed generalise the classical characterisation theorem for the category of algebras for a monad.

Example 2.9
.

Let
A
be a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category admitting a presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
\mathcal{P}A
, and let
T
be a
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched monad on
A
. Taking
j=1_{A}
, so that the nerve of
j
is the Yoneda embedding, the following diagram forms a pullback. This recovers the original characterisation of
[
Lin69
, Observation 1.1]
, as well as the enriched variant
[
Lin72
,
Str72
,
Lin74
]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Kl}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

One of the motivating applications of the nerve theorem for relative monads is to the theory of algebraic theories, in the sense of
[
Law63
, Chapter 2]
(cf.
[
AM25
, Example 5.6]
).

Example 2.10
.

Denote by
{\mathbb{F}}
the category of finite ordinals, which is the free category with strictly associative and unital finite coproducts on a single object
1
. The inclusion
j\colon{\mathbb{F}}\simeq{\mathbf{FinSet}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Set}}
of finite ordinals into sets exhibits a cocompletion under sifted colimits, and is hence dense and fully faithful.

Now consider a
j
-monad
T
. The inclusion of
\mathbb{F}
into the Kleisli category of
T
forms an algebraic theory
k_{T}\colon\mathbb{F}\to\mathbf{Kl}(T)
, since
k_{T}
is identity-on-objects and preserves finite coproducts. The category of
T
-algebras forms a pullback as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{\mathbb{F}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Noting that
\mathbf{Kl}(T)
has finite coproducts, the following also forms a pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cart}[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cart}[{\mathbb{F}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{\mathbb{F}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cart}[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Moreover, since
k_{T}
is identity-on-objects,
[k_{T}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is an amnestic isofibration, and so both pullbacks are furthermore bipullbacks
[
JS93
, Corollary 1]
. By the universal property of
{\mathbb{F}}
, there is an equivalence of categories
\mathbf{Cart}[{\mathbb{F}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\simeq{\mathbf{Set}}
concrete over
[{\mathbb{F}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. Consequently, the category of
T
-algebras is concretely equivalent to the category of finite product-preserving functors
\mathbf{Kl}(T){}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
, and thus to the category of algebras for the algebraic theory
k_{T}
.

Conversely, the relative monadicity theorem establishes that the category of models for an algebraic theory is strictly
j
-monadic
[
AM25
, Example 5.6]
. Together, these observations exhibit an equivalence between the category of algebraic theories and the category of
j
-monads, which commutes with taking categories of algebras (cf.
[
Ark22
, Chapter 3]
).

The analysis of
Example

2.10
extends to more general notions of algebraic theory; this perspective will be explicated in future work (cf.
[
Ark22
,
Ark22a
]
).

Another useful application of the nerve theorem is in establishing properties of the categories of algebras for relative monads: for instance, local presentability.

Example 2.11
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor with small domain and locally presentable codomain. The nerve
n_{j}\colon E\to\mathcal{P}A
has a left adjoint, since
E
is small-cocomplete. Therefore, for every
j
-monad
T
, each of the
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors appearing in the cospan of the pullback of
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories below is a right adjoint between locally presentable
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Kl}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Consequently, by
[
Bir84
, Theorem 6.11]
, the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
T
-algebras is locally presentable, and each of the functors above is a right adjoint. Furthermore, it follows from
[
AM25
, Proposition 4.12]
that
u_{T}
is strictly monadic.

In particular, if
A
is
\kappa
-cocomplete, for some regular cardinal
\kappa
, and
j
preserves
\kappa
-small colimits, then
n_{j}
is
\kappa
-accessible (i.e. preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits), in which case
\mathbf{Alg}(T)
is locally
\kappa
-presentable and each of the functors above is
\kappa
-accessible
[
Bir84
, Theorem 6.10]
.

An immediate consequence of
Example

2.11
is a simple proof that categories of algebras for accessible monads on locally presentable categories are themselves locally presentable
[
GU71
,
Ulm71
]
.

Example 2.12
.

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal and let
T
be a
\kappa
-accessible monad on a locally
\kappa
-presentable
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
E
. Denote by
j\colon A\to E
the full subcategory of
\kappa
-presentable objects in
E
. By
[
AM24
, Example 4.8]
, the forgetful
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor
u_{T}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to E
is strictly
j
-monadic. Thus, by
Example

2.11
,
\mathbf{Alg}(T)
is locally
\kappa
-presentable and
u_{T}
is
\kappa
-accessible.

Example 2.13
.

Example

2.10
and
Example

2.11
(for fixed
\kappa
) are instances of a more general phenomenon that holds for any class of weights that is
sound
in the sense of
[
ABL+02
,
LR11
]
. Let
\Psi
be a class of small weights and let
\Psi^{{+}}
be the class of
\Psi
-flat weights
[
KS05
]
. We say that
\Psi
is
sound
if, for every
\Psi
-cocomplete
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
A
, the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
\Psi
-exact
{\mathbb{V}}
-presheaves
\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}(A)
is equivalent to the free cocompletion
\Psi^{{+}}(A)
of
A
under
\Psi^{{+}}
-weighted colimits.

Let
A
be a small
\Psi
-cocomplete
{\mathbb{V}}
-category, and let
E
be a
locally
\Psi
-presentable
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
, i.e. the free
\Psi^{{+}}
-cocompletion of a small
\Psi
-cocomplete
{\mathbb{V}}
-category. Then, for any dense
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor
j\colon A\to E
, the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of algebras for any
j
-monad is also locally
\Psi
-presentable. We briefly sketch the proof idea; the full details will appear elsewhere.

Let
T
be a
j
-monad. The nerve theorem implies that the following diagram is a pullback of
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories; since
{k_{T}}^{*}
is an amnestic isofibration, it is also a bipullback
[
JS93
, Corollary 1]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Kl}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

By analogous arguments to
Example

2.11
,
n_{j}
and
{k_{T}}^{*}
preserve
\Psi
-flat colimits and admit left adjoints. Thus, it suffices to show that the 2-category of locally
\Psi
-presentable
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories and
\Psi^{{+}}
-cocontinuous right-adjoint functors admits bipullbacks, which will be preserved by the forgetful 2-functor into
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
. By a duality theorem for
\Psi
-complete
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories analogous to that of
[
CV02
, Theorem 3.1]
, this 2-category is dually biequivalent to the 2-category of small
\Psi
-complete
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories admitting absolute limits, which is the 2-category of algebras for an accessible 2-monad on
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, and hence admits bicolimits
[
BKP89
, Theorem 5.8]
. Since bilimits are preserved by biequivalence, the 2-category of locally
\Psi
-presentable
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories consequently admits bipullbacks, and thus the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
T
-algebras in the pullback above is locally
\Psi
-presentable.

In particular, when
{\mathbb{V}}={\mathbf{Set}}
and
\Psi
is the class of finite discrete weights, this recovers
Example

2.10
; and, when
\Psi
is the class of
\kappa
-small weights, recovers
Example

2.11
.

Next, we give an application of the nerve theorem to colimits of relative monads. Recall from
[
AM24
, Corollary 6.40]
that the assignment taking each
j
-monad
T
to its forgetful functor
u_{T}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to E
forms a fully faithful functor
u_{({-})}\colon\mathbf{RMnd}(j){}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E
. We extend the terminology of
[
Kel80
, §26]
from monads to relative monads.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
and
T_{{({-})}}\colon I\to\mathbf{RMnd}(j)
be functors.
A colimit of
T
is
algebraic
if it is preserved by
u_{({-})}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon\mathbf{RMnd}(j)\to({\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E){}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

In other words, given some functor
T\colon I\to\mathbf{RMnd}(j)
admitting a colimit
\operatorname{colim}_{i\in I}T_{i}
, the colimit is algebraic if it is sent by
u_{({-})}
to a limit in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E
. The following then generalises
[
Kel80
, Proposition 26.4]
from monads to relative monads with dense roots.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense functor with small domain. A diagram
{T\colon I\to\mathbf{RMnd}(j)}
of
j
-monads admits an algebraic colimit if and only if the limit
\operatorname{lim}_{i\in|I|}u_{T_{i}}
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E
admits a left
j
-adjoint.

Proof.

Denote by
u\colon D\to E
the limit
\operatorname{lim}_{i\in|I|}u_{T_{i}}
.
The functor
u_{({-})}\colon\mathbf{RMnd}(j){}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E
reflects limits because it is fully faithful, so it is enough to show that, if
u
admits a left
j
-adjoint, then it exhibits the category of algebras for the induced
j
-monad.

Denote by
k\colon A\to B
the colimit
\operatorname{colim}_{i\in|I|}k_{T_{i}}
in
A/{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
. The functor

[{({-})}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\colon A/{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}\to({\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]){}^{\mathrm{op}}

admits a right adjoint, and hence preserves colimits: this follows from the “preliminary structure–semantics adjunction” of
[
Lin69
, Theorem 3.1]
relative to the Yoneda embedding
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}\colon A\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
, observing that
A{}^{\mathrm{op}}/{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}\cong A/{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
.
We thus have that
[(\operatorname{colim}_{i\in|I|}k_{T_{i}}){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
exhibits the limit
\operatorname{lim}_{i\in|I|}[{k_{T_{i}}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. Now, for each
i\in|I|
, the nerve theorem implies that the following square is a pullback of categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{Kl}(T_{i}){}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[k_{T_{i}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since the pullback functor
n_{j}\times_{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}{({-})}\colon{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\to{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}/E
is right-adjoint (to postcomposition by
n_{j}
), it preserves limits, so that the following square also exhibits a pullback of categories.

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u=\operatorname{lim}_{i\in|I|}u_{T_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{lim}_{i\in|I|}[{k_{T_{i}}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]=[k{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

k
is identity-on-objects, so that
[k{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
strictly creates colimits. Thus
u
strictly creates those colimits preserved by
n_{j}
, i.e. the
j
-absolute colimits, by
[
Sch72
, Proposition 21.7.2(c)]
.
u
is therefore
j
-monadic if and only if it admits a left
j
-adjoint by
[
AM25
, Proposition 4.12]
.
∎

Example 2.16
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense functor with small domain and locally presentable codomain. Then each of the functors appearing in the cospan of the pullback (
4
) is a right adjoint between locally presentable categories, so that
u
is also right adjoint by
[
Bir84
, Theorem 6.11]
. Thus
\mathbf{RMnd}(j)
admits all small algebraic colimits.

As a final example, we explain how the nerve theorem for relative monads relates to the nerve theorems for monads arising in the theory of monads with arities
[
Web07
]
and nervous monads
[
BG19
]
.

Example 2.17

(Nerve theorems for monads)
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor with small domain and let
T
be a
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched monad on
E
. Precomposition by
j
defines a
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched
j
-monad
(j\mathbin{;}T)

[
AM24
, Proposition 5.36]
. The nerve theorem for relative monads implies that the following diagram is a pullback of
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(j\mathbin{;}T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Kl}(j\mathbin{;}T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j\mathbin{;}T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{j\mathbin{;}T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The Kleisli
{\mathbb{V}}
-category for
(j\mathbin{;}T)
is given by the full image of
(j\mathbin{;}k_{T})
, i.e. the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Kl}(T)
spanned by the objects of
A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Kl}(j\mathbin{;}T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Kl}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{j\mathbin{;}T}}

Now, assuming that
u_{j\mathbin{;}T}
admits a left adjoint,
[
AM25
, Proposition 4.12]
implies that
u_{j\mathbin{;}T}
is strictly monadic, and so the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
T
-algebras is isomorphic over
E
to the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
(j\mathbin{;}T)
-algebras.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(j\mathbin{;}T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j\mathbin{;}T}}

Monads satisfying this condition are called
j
-ary
in
[
Ark22
, Definition 5.4.1]
. In particular, monads with arities
j

[
Web07
, Definition 4.1]
and
j
-nervous monads
[
BG19
, Definition 17]
both satisfy the given assumptions, and are hence
j
-ary. We thereby recover the nerve theorems of
[
Web07
,
Mel10
,
BMW12
]
, and the characterisations of categories of algebras for nervous monads in
[
BG19
,
LP23
]
. In future work, we shall study the notion of
j
-ary monad in detail to clarify the precise relationship between these concepts (cf.
[
Ark22
, §5.4]
).

3.
Formal categorical prerequisites

We shall work throughout the remainder of paper in the context of a virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
, adopting the terminology and notation of
[
AM24
]
.
In this section, we shall briefly recall the fundamental concepts of formal category theory in a virtual equipment.
A more detailed introduction may be found in §2 – §3 of
[
AM24
]
.

A virtual equipment is, in particular, a virtual double category, which is a two-dimensional structure having
objects
;
tight-cells
(
\to
);
loose-cells
(
{\mathrel{\to}}
); and
2-cells
(
\Rightarrow
) of the following form (cf.
[
AM24
, Definition 2.1]
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

For instance, the virtual double category
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
has as objects the (possibly large)
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories; as tight-cells the
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors; as loose-cells the
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributors; and as 2-cells the
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformations, which are families of morphisms

\phi_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}}\colon p_{1}(x_{0},x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{n}(x_{n-1},x_{n})\to q(|f_{0}|x_{0},|f_{n}|x_{n})

in
{\mathbb{V}}
for each
x_{0}\in|A_{0}|,\dots,x_{n}\in|A_{n}|
, satisfying several naturality laws
[
AM24
, Definition 8.1]
. For tight-cells
f\colon A\to B
and
g\colon B\to C
, we denote by
(f\mathbin{;}g)\colon A\to C
or
gf\colon A\to C
their composite. For each pair of objects
A
and
B
in
\mathbb{X}
, the category of loose-cells
A{\mathrel{\to}}B
and globular 2-cells is denoted by
\mathbb{X}\llbracket A,B\rrbracket
, while the set of tight-cells
A\to B
is denoted by
\mathbb{X}[A,B]
.

A virtual equipment is a virtual double category for which every object
A
admits a loose-identity
A(1,1)\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
(
[
AM24
, Definition 2.4]
); and for which, for every loose-cell
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
and tight-cells
g\colon W\to X
and
f\colon Z\to Y
, there is a restriction loose-cell
p(f,g)\colon W{\mathrel{\to}}Z
(
[
AM24
, Definition 2.7]
). We denote the restriction
A(1,1)(f,g)
along a loose-identity
A(1,1)
by
A(f,g)
: in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, this restriction is given by the hom-objects of
A
. Finally, for each tight-cell
f\colon A\to B
, we denote by
\mathbin{\smallfrown_{f}}\colon A(1,1)\Rightarrow B(f,f)
and by
\mathbin{\smallsmile_{f}}\colon B(1,f),B(f,1)\Rightarrow A(1,1)
the unit and counit respectively of the loose-adjunction
B(1,f)\dashv B(f,1)

[
AM24
, Notation 2.8]
. In a virtual equipment, we may consider 2-cells between tight-cells, and consequently each set of tight-cells
\mathbb{X}[A,B]
extends to a category. A virtual equipment is representable if it admits composite of loose-cells; a representable virtual equipment is equivalently a pseudo double category with companions and conjoints.

3.1.
Relative monads, opalgebra objects and algebra objects

We recall the concepts introduced in
[
AM24
]
that are most relevant to our study of the nerve theorem. The central concept is that of a relative monad, which is a generalisation of a monad that permits the carrier to be an arbitrary tight-cell, rather than an endo-tight-cell. Accordingly, a relative monad is parameterised by a tight-cell
j\colon A\to E
that plays the same role that the identity tight-cell plays for a non-relative monad.

Definition 3.1

(
[
AM24
, Definition 4.1]
)
.

A
relative monad
comprises

(3.1.(1))

a tight-cell
j\colon A\to E
, the
root
;

(3.1.(2))

a tight-cell
t\colon A\to E
, the
carrier
;

(3.1.(3))

a 2-cell
{\dagger}\colon E(j,t)\Rightarrow E(t,t)
, the
extension operator
;

(3.1.(4))

a 2-cell
\eta\colon j\Rightarrow t
, the
unit
,

satisfying associativity and left- and right-unit laws.
A
j
-relative monad
(or simply
j
-monad
) is a relative monad with root
j
.

Relative monads admit notions of algebra and algebra morphism analogous to those for non-relative monads. However, it is important to observe that, in the context of a virtual equipment, the appropriate notion of morphism of algebras is graded by a chain of loose-cells. This permits the consideration of morphisms between algebras with different domains, in contrast to morphisms of algebras in a 2-category
[
Str72
]
.

Definition 3.2

(
[
AM24
, Definitions 6.1 & 6.29]
)
.

Let
T
be a relative monad. An
algebra for
T
(or simply
T
-algebra
) comprises

(3.2.(1))

an object
D
, the
domain
;

(3.2.(2))

a tight-cell
e\colon D\to E
, the
carrier
;

(3.2.(3))

a 2-cell
{\rtimes}\colon E(j,e)\Rightarrow E(t,e)
, the
extension operator
,

satisfying compatibility laws with the extension operator
{\dagger}
and unit
\eta
for
T
.

Let
(e\colon D\to E,{\rtimes})
and
(e^{\prime}\colon D^{\prime}\to E,{\rtimes}^{\prime})
be
T
-algebras. A
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
-graded
T
-algebra morphism
from
(e,{\rtimes})
to
(e^{\prime},{\rtimes}^{\prime})
is a 2-cell

\epsilon\colon E(1,e),p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})

satisfying a compatibility law with the two extension operators
{\rtimes}
and
{\rtimes}^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.3

(
[
AM24
, Remark 6.30]
)
.

A
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
-graded
T
-algebra morphism from
(e,{\rtimes})
to
(e^{\prime},{\rtimes}^{\prime})
is equivalently a 2-cell

\epsilon\colon p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\Rightarrow E(e,e^{\prime})

satisfying a compatibility law with the two extension operators
{\rtimes}
and
{\rtimes}^{\prime}
. We shall use the term
algebra morphism
to refer to both forms of 2-cell interchangeably.

An algebra object for a relative monad
T
is a universal
T
-algebra. In
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, this corresponds to the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
T
-algebras
[
AM24
, §8.3]
.

Definition 3.4

(
[
AM24
, Definition 6.33]
)
.

Let
T
be a relative monad. A
T
-algebra
(u_{T}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)\to E,{\rtimes}_{T})
is an

algebra object
for
T
when

(3.4.(1))

for every
T
-algebra
(e\colon D\to E,{\rtimes})
, there is a
unique tight-cell
{\langle\rangle}_{(e,{\rtimes})}\colon D\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
such that
{\langle\rangle}_{(e,{\rtimes})}\mathbin{;}u_{T}=e
and
{\rtimes}_{T}(1,{\langle\rangle}_{(e,{\rtimes})})={\rtimes}
;

(3.4.(2))

for every graded
T
-algebra morphism
\epsilon\colon E(1,e),p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})
, there is a unique 2-cell
{\langle\rangle}_{\epsilon}\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)(1,{\langle\rangle}_{(e,{\rtimes})}),p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{Alg}(T)(1,{\langle\rangle}_{(e^{\prime},{\rtimes}^{\prime})})
such that:

\epsilon\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox to181.82pt{\vbox to58.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 90.91095pt\lower-30.7287pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Definition 3.5

(
[
AM24
, Definitions 6.4 & 6.43]
)
.

Let
T
be a relative monad. An
opalgebra for
T
(or simply
T
-opalgebra
) comprises

(3.5.(1))

an object
B
, the
codomain
;

(3.5.(2))

a tight-cell
a\colon A\to B
, the
carrier
or
underlying tight-cell
;

(3.5.(3))

a 2-cell
{\ltimes}\colon E(j,t)\Rightarrow B(a,a)
, the
extension operator
,

satisfying compatibility laws with the extension operator
{\dagger}
and unit
\eta
for
T
.

Let
(a\colon A\to B,{\ltimes})
and
(a^{\prime}\colon A\to B^{\prime},{\ltimes}^{\prime})
be
T
-opalgebras. A
(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
-graded
T
-opalgebra morphism
from
(a,{\ltimes})
to
(a^{\prime},{\ltimes}^{\prime})
is a 2-cell

\alpha\colon p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},B(1,a)\Rightarrow B^{\prime}(1,a^{\prime})

satisfying a compatibility law with the two extension operators
{\ltimes}
and
{\ltimes}^{\prime}
.

An opalgebra object for a relative monad
T
is a universal
T
-opalgebra. In
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, this corresponds to the Kleisli
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
[
AM24
, §8.4]
.

Definition 3.6

(
[
AM24
, Definition 6.45]
)
.

Let
T
be a relative monad. A
T
-opalgebra
(k_{T}\colon A\to\mathbf{Opalg}(T),{\ltimes}_{T})
is called an

opalgebra object
for
T
when

(3.6.(1))

for every
T
-opalgebra
(a\colon A\to B,{\ltimes})
, there is a unique tight-cell
[]_{(a,{\ltimes})}\colon\mathbf{Opalg}(T)\to B
such that
k_{T}\mathbin{;}[]_{(a,{\ltimes})}=a
and
{\ltimes}_{T}\mathbin{;}[]_{(a,{\ltimes})}={\ltimes}
;

(3.6.(2))

for every graded
T
-opalgebra morphism
\alpha\colon p_{1},\dots,p_{n},B(1,a)\Rightarrow B^{\prime}(1,a^{\prime})
, there is a unique 2-cell
[]_{\alpha}\colon p_{1},\dots,p_{n},B(1,[]_{(a,{\ltimes})})\Rightarrow B^{\prime}(1,[]_{(a^{\prime},{\ltimes}^{\prime})})
such that:

\alpha\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox to180.43pt{\vbox to58.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 90.21167pt\lower-30.7287pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

For every virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
, there is a dual virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}{}^{\mathrm{co}}
. Results about relative comonads and their coalgebras will consequently follow from the results in this paper by duality
[
AM24
, §7]
; we shall not state these dual formulations explicitly.

3.2.
Loose-monads

A loose-monad on an object
A
in a virtual double category is a monoid in the multicategory of loose-cells
A{\mathrel{\to}}A
. Loose-monads in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
have also been called
promonads
,
profunctor monads
, or
arrows
(cf.
Remark

8.3
). Loose-monads in a given virtual double category form a virtual double category.

Definition 3.7

(
[
Lei04
, Definition 5.3.1]
)
.

\mathbb{X}
be a virtual double category. The virtual double category
{\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}(\mathbb{X})}

Remark 3.8
.

In
[
CS10
, Definition 2.8]
, in which virtual double categories are named after their loose-cells rather than their objects,
{\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}(\mathbb{X})}
is called
{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}od}}(\mathbb{X})
.

For a fixed object
A\in\mathbb{X}
, we denote by
\textup{\sagittarius}\mathbf{Mnd}(A)
the category of loose-monads on
A

[
AM24
, Definition 2.16]
.

4.
The loose-monad associated to a relative monad

We now proceed with our pursuit of a more abstract understanding of the nerve theorem. This begins with the understanding of relative monads in terms of loose-monads. It is shown in
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.22]
that, given a
j
-monad
T
with carrier
t\colon A\to E
, the loose-cell
E(j,t)\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
has the structure of a loose-monad. In this section we sharpen this result by showing that if
E(j,t)
has the structure of a loose-monad, and satisfies a mild compatibility condition, the tight-cell
t\colon A\to E
has the structure of a
j
-monad. This establishes a strong relationship between a relative monad
T
and its associated loose-monad
E(j,T)
, which we will build upon in the subsequent sections.

To begin, we shall need an abstract result about monoids in multicategories (
Proposition

4.7
). We will then specialise to the multicategory of endo-loose-cells on a fixed object in a virtual equipment to obtain a statement about relative monads (
Proposition

4.9
).

Definition 4.1
.

A morphism
g\colon B\to C
in a multicategory is
monic
if, for each pair of multimorphisms
f,f^{\prime}\colon A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\to B
, we have
(f\mathbin{;}g=f^{\prime}\mathbin{;}g)\implies(f=f^{\prime})
.

Example 4.2
.

For each section–retraction pair
s\colon B\rightleftarrows A\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mur
(i.e. satisfying
(s\mathbin{;}r)=1_{B}
), the section
s
is monic.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{M}}
be a multicategory and let
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
be a monoid in
{\mathbf{M}}
. Let
B
be an object together with a morphism
s\colon B\to A
. If
s
is monic, then there is at most one monoid structure on
B
for which
s
is a monoid morphism.

Proof.

Suppose that
B
is equipped with two monoid structures
(\mu_{B},\eta_{B})
and
(\mu^{\prime}_{B},\eta^{\prime}_{B})
for which
s
is a monoid morphism, so that the following diagrams commute. Consequently, since
s
is monic,
\mu_{B}=\mu^{\prime}_{B}
and
\eta_{B}=\eta^{\prime}_{B}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

∎

In particular, given a section–retraction pair
s\colon B\rightleftarrows A\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mur
, if
B
admits a monoid structure for which
s
is a monoid morphism, the monoid structure is necessarily given by
B,B\xrightarrow{s,s}A,A\xrightarrow{\mu_{A}}A\xrightarrow{r}B
and
{}\xrightarrow{\eta_{A}}A\xrightarrow{r}B
. It is natural to ask when this canonical structure does indeed form a monoid. With this in mind, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
be a monoid in a multicategory. An
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
-section
comprises a section–retraction pair
s\colon B\rightleftarrows A\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mur
rendering the following diagram commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}

Example 4.5
.

Let
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
be a monoid in a multicategory and let
e\colon A\to A
be an idempotent monoid homomorphism. If
e
admits a splitting
(r\mathbin{;}s)\colon A\to B\to A
, then
(s,r)
forms an
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
-section.

Example 4.6
.

If a multicategory admits a unit
\eta_{J}\colon{}\to J
, then
J
carries a canonical monoid structure (cf.
[
AM24
, Proposition 4.12]
).
Up to isomorphism, the only section of this monoid is
J
itself.
Indeed, for each section
(s,r)
of the monoid
J
, the equation
(\eta_{J}\mathbin{;}r\mathbin{;}s)=\eta_{J}
implies that
(r\mathbin{;}s)
is the identity on
J
, so that
s
is an isomorphism of monoids.

Intuitively, the two commutativity laws in
Definition

4.4
are exactly those stating that
s
is a monoid morphism to
A
from the tentative monoid structure on
B
. The following proposition shows that, under these assumptions, the tentative monoid structure is indeed a monoid structure.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{M}}
be a multicategory and let
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
be a monoid in
{\mathbf{M}}
. An
(A,\mu_{A},\eta_{A})
-section
(B,s,r)
endows
B
with a unique monoid structure such that
s
is a monoid morphism.

Note that
r
need not be a monoid morphism (though does preserve the unit, by definition).

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.3
, the unit and multiplication are uniquely determined. Define

\displaystyle\eta_{B}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}{}\xrightarrow{\eta_{A}}A\xrightarrow{r}B

\displaystyle\mu_{B}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}B,B\xrightarrow{s,s}A,A\xrightarrow{\mu_{A}}A\xrightarrow{r}B

We will show that
(B,\mu_{B},\eta_{B})
is a monoid. The unit laws follow from commutativity of the following diagrams, since
s
is monic.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B},B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A},A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,\eta_{A}}

The associativity law follows from commutativity of the following diagram, since
s
is monic.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B},B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A},A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

s
is then a monoid morphism, the unit law following from the unit section law; and the multiplication law following from the multiplication section law.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Ralph Sarkis and Jean-Baptiste Vienney have observed that there is a complementary notion of
monoid retract
, which implies that
r
, rather than
s
, is a monoid morphism.

Recall that, for any object
A
in a virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
, the endo-loose-cells
A{\mathrel{\to}}A
form a multicategory
\mathbb{X}\llbracket A,A\rrbracket
. Every tight-cell
t\colon A\to E
induces a monoid
E(t,t)
in this multicategory. The insight relating relative monads to loose-monads is the observation that the laws for a
j
-relative monad with carrier
t
are precisely the laws for a section of the monoid
E(t,t)
. Thus, every relative monad is a section of an endomorphism monoid. This may be viewed as a Cayley representation for relative monads, though it is sharper than the typical formulations of representation theorems (cf.
[
RJ17
]
), as the laws for the submonoid are deduced rather than assumed.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
t\colon A\to E
be a tight-cell in a virtual equipment with chosen restrictions. The following are in bijection.

(4.9.(1))

Relative monads with carrier
t
.

(4.9.(2))

E(t,t)
-sections with strictly
t
-corepresentable carrier.

Proof.

Let
(B,s,r)
be an
E(t,t)
-section. Strict
t
-corepresentability means that
B
is of the form
E(j,t)
for some tight-cell
j\colon A\to E
(it is this that requires that we have chosen restrictions, since otherwise it is only possible to consider corepresentability up to isomorphism). We have an induced 2-cell
{}\xRightarrow{\mathbin{\smallfrown_{t}}}E(t,t)\xRightarrow{r}E(j,t)
, which is equivalent to a 2-cell
j\Rightarrow t
, that we shall call
\eta
. Defining
{\dagger}\mathrel{:=}s
, we therefore have a section–retraction pair

E(j,t)\xRightarrow{{\dagger}}E(t,t)\xRightarrow{E(\eta,t)}E(j,t)

which gives us the root, unit, and first unit law for a relative monad.

For the second unit law, observe that the following diagram commutes (as is particularly clear from the corresponding string diagrams).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(t,t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(j,j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(j,t)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\eta,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,\eta)}

Therefore the unit law for the section is the second unit law for a relative monad. For the associativity law, observe that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\mathbin{;}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s,A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

In our case, the bottom composite is

E(j,t),E(t,t)\xRightarrow{{\dagger},E(t,t)}E(t,t),E(t,t)\xRightarrow{\mathbin{\smallsmile_{t}}(t,t)}E(t,t)\xRightarrow{E(\eta,t)}E(j,t)

which is equal to
\mathbin{\smallfrown_{t}}(j,t)
by the first unit law of the relative monad. Thus, the multiplication law for the section is the associativity law for the relative monad.

For the converse, every
j
-relative monad with carrier
t
induces an
E(t,t)
-section
(E(j,t),{\dagger},E(\eta,t))
.
∎

Note that we do not recover a relationship between
j
-monad morphisms and loose-monad morphisms this way, since it is
j
that varies above, rather than
t
. However, we shall not be concerned with the morphisms for what follows.

Remark 4.10
.

Dually, for a tight-cell
d\colon Z\to U
, relative comonads are the
U(d,d)
-sections with strictly
d
-representable carriers.

We observe in passing that, as a consequence of
Proposition

4.9
, the associativity law in
[
AM24
, Definition 4.1]
can be replaced by the following equation.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

For relative monads in
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
, this says that the associativity law is equivalently specified by asking that, given morphisms
f\colon|j|x\to|t|y
and
g\colon|j|y\to|t|z
, if we first form the composite
(f^{\dagger}\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger})\colon|t|x\to|t|y\to|t|z
by extending both morphisms, then, subsequently, precomposing the unit and then extending does nothing. In other words, the following equation holds.

(\eta_{x}\mathbin{;}f^{\dagger}\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}\mathbin{;}g^{\dagger}

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
T
be a
j
-monad. Then
E(j,T)
is a loose-monad, and
{\dagger}\colon E(j,t)\Rightarrow E(t,t)
is a loose-monad morphism.

This statement follows from
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.22 & Example 6.6 & Lemma 6.7]
. However, the formulation of this section facilitates a direct proof.

Proof.

Direct from
Propositions

4.7
and
4.7
.
∎

5.
Exact virtual equipments

In our previous work on the formal theory of relative monads
[
AM24
,
AM25
]
, we worked within the setting of a virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
, without additional global assumptions. In contrast, to establish the nerve theorem formally, we require
\mathbb{X}
to admit more structure. Conceptually, the relevant structure is an exactness property, corresponding to the existence of certain colimits that interact well with restrictions (which may be thought of as a limit-like notion). While not every virtual equipment satisfies this property, it is commonly satisfied in settings of interest for formal category theory. For instance, it is satisfied in every virtual equipment constructed from a virtual double category with restrictions via the loose-monads construction
\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}
, which includes virtual equipments of enriched categories, internal categories, generalised multicategories, and so on
[
CS10
]
.

Before introducing the definition of exactness for a virtual equipment, we first make a small simplification to our setting, motivated by the observation that, while restrictions in a general virtual double category are identified up to isomorphism by their universal property, in many examples of interest there is a canonical choice of restrictions that are particularly well behaved. For instance, in the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
of
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched categories, restriction is given by pre- and postcomposition of
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors, and is consequently strictly functorial (rather than being only pseudofunctorial, as is automatic for restrictions by their universal property). This is convenient, as it means that we may often reason about loose-cells in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
up to equality, rather than up to isomorphism. The following definition captures this property.

Definition 5.1
.

A virtual equipment is
strict
if equipped with a strictly functorial choice of restrictions, in the sense that following 2-cells exhibit the chosen cartesian 2-cells (so that
p(1,1)=p
and
p(f,g)(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})=p(ff^{\prime},gg^{\prime})
), for all objects
A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime\prime}
, tight-cells
f,f^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}
, and loose-cell
p
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(ff^{\prime},gg^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

We shall henceforth assume our ambient virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
is strict. While this is not a necessary assumption (that is, our theorems continue to hold, in a weaker sense, without the assumption of strictness), it significantly simplifies reasoning that involves restrictions. For instance, without this assumption, we would have to consider in places universal properties that identify objects only up to equivalence, rather than isomorphism (cf.
Remark

7.3
). Given that examples of interest are strict, this seems a reasonable trade-off. Lest the reader worry that we are sacrificing generality for simplicity, we note that strictness is not a restrictive assumption, as every virtual equipment is equivalent to a strict one (
Theorem

A.1
).

We may now introduce the appropriate notion of exactness for a virtual equipment. Note that we shall not assume our ambient virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
is exact throughout the rest of the paper: exactness will be explicitly assumed when it is needed.

Definition 5.2
.

A strict virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
is
exact
when the following conditions hold.

(5.2.(1))

For every object
A
in
\mathbb{X}
and loose-monad
T
on
A
, there is an object
\textup{«}T\textup{»}
and tight-cell
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T}\colon A\to\textup{«}T\textup{»}
, the
collapse
of
T
,
satisfying
T=\textup{«}T\textup{»}(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T})
and
such that, for every object
B
in
\mathbb{X}
and loose-monad morphism
(f,\phi)\colon T\to B(1,1)
, there is a unique tight-cell
[]_{f}\colon\textup{«}T\textup{»}\to B
factoring
(f,\phi)
.

\phi\quad=\quad\hbox to122.98pt{\vbox to81.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.48735pt\lower-40.31255pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(5.2.(2))

For every object
A
in
\mathbb{X}
, the identity on
1_{A}
exhibits the collapse of the loose-identity
A(1,1)
.

(5.2.(3))

For every loose-monad module
p\colon T^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}T
in
\mathbb{X}
, there is a loose-cell
\textup{«}p\textup{»}\colon\textup{«}T^{\prime}\textup{»}{\mathrel{\to}}\textup{«}T\textup{»}
, the
collapse
of
p
,
satisfying
p=\textup{«}p\textup{»}(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}})
and
such that, for every loose-monad transformation,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}(1,1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

there is a unique 2-cell
[]_{\psi}
factoring
\psi
.
