\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(5.2.(4))

For every loose-monad
T
in
\mathbb{X}
, the collapse of
T
qua a loose-monad exhibits the collapse of
T
qua an identity loose-monad module
T{\mathrel{\to}}T
.

(5.2.(5))

Each loose-cell
p\colon\textup{«}T^{\prime}\textup{»}{\mathrel{\to}}\textup{«}T\textup{»}
between collapses is equal to the collapse
\textup{«}p(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}})\textup{»}
of the loose-monad module
p(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}})\colon T^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}T
.

Remark 5.3
.

Definition

5.2
is inspired by that of
[
Sch15
, Definition 5.1]
(which, in turn, is inspired by
[
Woo85
]
’s Axiom 5
[
Woo85
, §2]
and its subsequent study in
[
GS16
, §15 & §16]
).
[
Sch15
]
motivates the terminology by drawing a number of connections between exact virtual equipments and exact categories in the sense of
[
Bar71
]
. However, the definition of
[
Sch15
]
is too weak, as it specifies a universal property only for unary 2-cells. (The unary property is sufficient for representable virtual equipments, which is
[
Sch15
]
’s primary interest.) Note that our universal property
Item

5.2.(3)
for multiary 2-cells can only be defined in the presence of collapses for
all
loose-monad modules. In contrast,
[
Sch15
, Definition 3.9]
gives a definition of collapse for an individual loose-monad module that does not require the existence of collapses for other loose-monad modules, but is consequently too weak to prove theorems of interest (e.g.
Theorem

6.2
).

Remark 5.4
.

Abstractly,
Definition

5.2
corresponds to asking for the inclusion
\mathbb{X}\to{\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}(\mathbb{X})}
of
\mathbb{X}
into the virtual equipment of loose-monads and modules in
\mathbb{X}
– which sends each object
A
to the loose-monad
A(1,1)
– to admit a left-adjoint retraction
\textup{«}{-}\textup{»}\colon{\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}(\mathbb{X})}\to\mathbb{X}
that strictly preserves loose-identities and restrictions, and whose unit components are cartesian. This condition is equivalent to asking for
\mathbb{X}
to be a well-behaved algebra for the
\textup{\sagittarius}{\mathbf{\mathbb{M}nd}}
construction (cf.
[
CS10
,
Woo85
]
). However, we shall defer this perspective to future work.

6.
Algebras and opalgebras from exactness

In
Section

4
, we showed that
j
-relative monads are essentially characterised by loose-monads of the form
E(j,T)
. An essential aspect of the theory of relative monads is the theory of algebras and opalgebras
[
AM24
, §6]
. In this section, we shall show that the relationship between a
j
-monad
T
and its associated loose-monad
E(j,T)
extends to algebras and opalgebras: in other words, that we may characterise both the
T
-algebras and the
T
-opalgebras in terms of
E(j,T)
. In particular, this characterisation extends to the universal algebras and universal opalgebras, which in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
are precisely the notions of Eilenberg–Moore
{\mathbb{V}}
-category, and Kleisli
{\mathbb{V}}
-category. This has several useful consequences: for instance, we shall show that it is possible to construct algebra objects and opalgebra objects in a virtual equipment with suitable limits and colimits (
Theorems

6.2
and
6.8
). In particular, the construction of an algebra object in this manner generalises the nerve theorem to settings in which presheaf categories may not exist.

6.1.
Opalgebra objects via collapse

We begin by considering opalgebras. In addition to being the simpler of the two to characterise, the description of algebra objects in terms of
E(j,T)
involves the corresponding description of opalgebras.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a tight-cell and let
T
be a
j
-monad. For each tight-cell
a\colon A\to B
, there are bijections between

•

T
-opalgebra structures on
a
, and loose-monad morphisms
E(j,T)\Rightarrow B(a,a)
;

•

(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
-graded
T
-opalgebra morphisms as on the left below, and loose-monad transformations as on the right below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}(1,a^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(1,a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(j,T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}(1,1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(1,a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}(a^{\prime},1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Proof.

The correspondence between opalgebras and loose-monad morphisms is
[
AM24
, Lemma 6.7]
. In the correspondence between opalgebra morphisms and loose-monad transformations, the only nontrivial law is the compatibility between the action of
B(1,a)
and the action of
B^{\prime}(a^{\prime},1)
, which corresponds to the single compatibility condition for an opalgebra morphism.
∎

Consequently, it is natural to expect that opalgebra objects for
T
are characterised by universal loose-monad morphisms from
E(j,T)
. This is where exactness enters the picture (
Definition

5.2
). The universal properties of opalgebra objects (
[
AM24
, Definition 6.45]
) and of collapses are closely related. First, the distinction between their one-dimensional universal properties is exactly the respective distinction between loose-monad morphisms of the form on the left below, and loose-monad morphisms of the form on the right below. It is straightforward to confirm that the two forms are in bijection, and so opalgebra objects and collapses satisfy the same one-dimensional universal property.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(f,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Second, the distinction between the two-dimensional universal properties is the distinction between a “one-sided” universal property in the case of opalgebra objects, which involves a chain of loose-cells stemming from a single opalgebra object, and a “multivariant” universal property in the case of collapses, which involves a chain of loose-cells between multiple collapses. Thus, collapses satisfy a stronger universal property than opalgebra objects (indeed, this is the reason we must continue to work with collapses in
Section

6.2
, rather than opalgebra objects, as we shall have need of this stronger universal property). The preceding discussion provides the intuition for the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a tight-cell and let
T
be a
j
-monad. If
\mathbb{X}
is exact, then the coprojection
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{E(j,T)}\colon A\to\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}
exhibits an opalgebra object for
T
.

Proof.

For convenience, we shall denote by

\pi

the tight-cell
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{E(j,T)}
. We shall show that the canonical opcartesian 2-cell
E(j,t)\Rightarrow\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}})
exhibits the opalgebra object.

By definition, we have that
E(j,T)\Rightarrow\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}})
is a loose-monad morphism, hence is an opalgebra by
Lemma

6.1
. Let
(a,{\ltimes})
be a
T
-opalgebra, i.e. a loose-monad morphism
{E(j,T)\Rightarrow B(a,a)}
. The universal property of the collapse gives a unique factorisation of

through

\pi

, and
{\ltimes}
through the cartesian 2-cell defining the collapse, as below on the left. This is equivalent to a unique factorisation of
{\ltimes}
through the induced opcartesian 2-cell (by definition of opcartesianness), as below on the right, and hence exhibits the one-dimensional universal property of an opalgebra object.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle\pi

\scriptstyle\pi

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[]_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[]_{a}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{opcart}}

.

, we have

, and so this is equivalent to a factorisation of

.
