∎

As a consequence, every exact virtual equipment admits opalgebra objects for relative monads. However, we shall see that, since the universal property of a collapse is stronger than that of an opalgebra object, opalgebra objects in exact equipments are particularly well behaved.

6.2.
Algebra objects via collapse

We now consider algebras. In contrast to the consideration of opalgebras in
Section

6.1
, it will be necessary here to assume that
j
is dense. We first observe that
T
-algebras and their graded morphisms may be characterised in terms of the loose-monad
E(j,T)
. In contrast to opalgebras (
Lemma

6.1
), algebras may not be characterised in terms of loose-monad morphisms: instead, they may be characterised in terms of loose-monad modules. (Note that, by taking companions, we could also have characterised
T
-opalgebras in terms of loose-monad modules
E(j,T){\mathrel{\to}}B(1,1)
.)

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense tight-cell and let
T
be a
j
-monad. For each tight-cell
e\colon D\to E
, there are bijections between

•

T
-algebra structures on
e
, and loose-monad modules
D(1,1){\mathrel{\to}}E(j,T)
with carrier
E(j,e)\colon D{\mathrel{\to}}A
;

•

(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
-graded
T
-algebra morphisms as on the left below, and loose-monad transformations as on the right below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(1,e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(1,e^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(j,T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(j,T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}(1,1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,e^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Proof.

A
T
-algebra structure on
e
is a 2-cell
E(j,e)\Rightarrow E(t,e)
compatible with the unit and extension operators of
T
.
A 2-cell
E(j,e)\Rightarrow E(t,e)
is equivalently a 2-cell
E(1,t),E(j,e)\Rightarrow E(1,e)
, and hence, by density of
j
, equivalently a 2-cell
E(j,t),E(j,e)\Rightarrow E(j,e)
.
The unit and extension laws for a
T
-algebra then correspond to the unit and multiplication laws of a loose-monad module.

Similarly, 2-cells
E(1,e),p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})
are, by density of
j
, in bijection with 2-cells
E(j,e),p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\Rightarrow E(j,e^{\prime})
.
The former is a
T
-algebra morphism exactly when the latter is a loose-monad transformation.
∎

Perhaps surprisingly, the asymmetry between algebras and opalgebras means that universal algebras are characterised quite differently (with respect to the associated loose-monad) to universal opalgebras. In fact, universal algebras are characterised in terms of universal opalgebras, together with the following virtual double categorical limit notion.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
n\colon E{\mathrel{\to}}A
be a loose-cell and let
k\colon A\to K
be a tight-cell.
A
semanticiser
of
k
relative to
n
comprises an object
n\times_{A}k
, a tight-cell
\pi_{1}\colon n\times_{A}k\to E
, and a loose-cell
\pi_{2}\colon n\times_{A}k{\mathrel{\to}}K
satisfying
\pi_{2}(k,1)=n(1,\pi_{1})
, which is universal in the following sense.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n\times_{A}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

(6.4.(1))

For each tight-cell
e\colon\cdot\to E
and loose-cell
p\colon\cdot{\mathrel{\to}}K
such that
p(k,1)=n(1,e)
, there is a unique tight-cell
\langle e,p\rangle\colon\cdot\to n\times_{A}k
such that
\langle e,p\rangle\mathbin{;}\pi_{1}=e
and
\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)=p
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n\times_{A}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle e,p\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

(6.4.(2))

For each pair of 2-cells

\chi_{1}\colon E(1,e),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})\qquad\chi_{2}\colon p,s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow p^{\prime}\qquad(n\geq 0)

that induce the same 2-cell
n(1,e),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow n(1,e^{\prime})
by composition with
n
and
K(k,1)
respectively, there is a unique 2-cell

\langle\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\rangle\colon({n\times_{A}k})(1,\langle e,p\rangle),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow({n\times_{A}k})(1,\langle e^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle)

that induces
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
by composition with
E(1,\pi_{1})
and
\pi_{2}
respectively.

Remark 6.5
.

The name
semanticiser
is intended to be suggestive of the operation of taking the semantics of an algebraic theory in
[
Law63
]
’s structure–semantics adjunction and in
[
Lin69
]
’s subsequent generalisation. We will expand upon this connection in future work.

We observe that
Definition

6.4
simplifies in examples of interest. First, we must introduce the notion of density for a loose-cell.

Definition 6.6
.

A loose-cell
p\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}B
is
dense
when the identity 2-cell
1_{p}\colon p\Rightarrow p
exhibits
A(1,1)
as the right lift
p\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p
.

Observe that a tight-cell
j\colon A\to E
is dense in the sense of
[
AM24
, Definition 3.19]
if and only if its conjoint
E(j,1)\colon E{\mathrel{\to}}A
is dense in the sense of
Definition

6.6
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider a square of the following shape, such that
\pi_{2}(k,1)=n(1,e)
and in which
n
is dense.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n\times_{A}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Then
Item

6.4.(2)
is equivalent to the condition that, for every pair of tight-cells
e
and
e^{\prime}
with codomain
E
, every pair of loose-cells
p
and
p^{\prime}
with codomain
K
, and every 2-cell

\chi_{2}\colon p,s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow p^{\prime}\qquad(n\geq 0)

such that
p(k,1)=n(1,e)
and
p^{\prime}(k,1)=n(1,e^{\prime})
, there is a unique 2-cell

({n\times_{A}k})(1,\langle e,p\rangle),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow({n\times_{A}k})(1,\langle e^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle)

that induces
\chi_{2}
by composition with
\pi_{2}
.
Consequently, the above square is a semanticiser if and only if it satisfies
Item

6.4.(1)
and
\pi_{2}
is dense.

Proof.

Each 2-cell
\chi_{2}\colon p,s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow p^{\prime}
induces a 2-cell
n(1,e),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow n(1,e^{\prime})
by postcomposing
K(k,1)
, but these are in bijection, by density of
n
, with 2-cells
E(1,e),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})
.
Hence, in
Item

6.4.(2)
, the 2-cell
\chi_{2}
uniquely determines
\chi_{1}
, and the equivalence follows.

Assuming the square satisfies
Item

6.4.(1)
, it suffices to consider the case in which
{e=e^{\prime}=\pi_{1}}
and
p=p^{\prime}=\pi_{2}
; the general case follows by expressing
p
and
p^{\prime}
as restrictions of
\pi_{2}
.
The required condition then expresses a bijection between 2-cells
\pi_{2},s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow\pi_{2}
and 2-cells
{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow({n\times_{A}k})(1,1)}
; this bijection is precisely density of
\pi_{2}
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove our main result, characterising the algebra object for a relative monad in terms of its associated loose-monad and its opalgebra object
k_{T}\colon A\to\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense tight-cell and let
T
be a
j
-monad.
If
\mathbb{X}
is exact, then a tight-cell
u\colon D\to E
exhibits the algebra object for
T
if and only if there exists a loose-cell
\pi\colon D{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
exhibiting the following square as a semanticiser.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Opalg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Proof.

The tight-cell
k_{T}\colon A\to\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
exhibits the collapse of
E(j,T)
by
Theorem

6.2
, and, by
Lemma

6.3
, we can view
T
-algebras and their morphisms equivalently as loose-monad modules and transformations.

To give a loose-cell
\pi\colon D{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
such that
\pi(k_{T},1)=E(j,u)
is thus equivalently to give a
T
-algebra with carrier
u
.
We show that such a
T
-algebra is the algebra object exactly when the square above is a semanticiser.

A
j
-representable loose-monad module
E(j,e)\colon D(1,1){\mathrel{\to}}T
is equivalently a loose-cell
\textup{«}E(j,e)\textup{»}\colon D{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
, by taking the collapse.
Given this data, the universal properties of the semanticiser and of the algebra object both ask for a unique tight-cells
{\langle\rangle}_{e}\colon\cdot\to D
such that
e={\langle\rangle}_{d}\mathbin{;}u
.
The semanticiser additionally requires
\pi(1,{\langle\rangle}_{e})=\textup{«}E(j,e)\textup{»}
, while the algebra object additionally requires
E(j,e)
to be the module induced by restricting
E(j,u)
along
{\langle\rangle}_{e}
.
These two conditions are equivalent because the collapse of the latter loose-module is
\pi(1,{\langle\rangle}_{e})
.

Let
E(j,e)
and
E(j,e^{\prime})\colon D(1,1){\mathrel{\to}}T
be
j
-representable loose-monad modules.
A loose-monad transformation
E(j,e),s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(j,e^{\prime})
is equivalently a 2-cell
\chi_{2}\colon\textup{«}E(j,e)\textup{»},s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow\textup{«}E(j,e^{\prime})\textup{»}
.
By
Lemma

6.7
it follows that the two-dimensional universal properties of the semanticiser and of the algebra object are equivalent.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

From
Theorem

6.8
, taking
j=1
and assuming
\mathbb{X}
is representable, we recover
[
Woo85
, Proposition 7]
, in which semanticisers relative to
A(1,1)
are called
universal kones
.

Recall that the opalgebra and algebra objects for a
j
-monad
T
form initial and terminal resolutions
k_{T}\mathrel{\dashv_{j}}v_{T}
and
f_{T}\mathrel{\dashv_{j}}u_{T}
respectively
[
AM24
, Corollary 6.41 & Corollary 6.51]
. As a consequence, they are related by a unique morphism of resolutions
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Opalg}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)

[
AM24
, Definition 5.23 & Remark 6.54]
, the comparison tight-cell, which renders the following diagram commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Opalg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}

What is remarkable about
Theorem

6.8
is that, in the setting of exact virtual equipments, it exhibits a
joint
universal property of the opalgebra and algebra objects, mediated by the comparison tight-cell. As a consequence, in this setting,
i_{T}
is particularly well behaved. We summarise this observation in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense tight-cell and let
T
be a
j
-monad admitting an algebra object.
Assume that
\mathbb{X}
is exact.
Then the loose-cell
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T},1)\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T){\mathrel{\to}}\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
is isomorphic to a loose-cell
\pi
that exhibits the following square as a semanticiser.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Opalg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(j,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Consequently, the comparison tight-cell
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Opalg}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
is dense and fully faithful.

Proof.

Since
k_{T}\colon A\to\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
exhibits the collapse of the loose-monad
E(j,T)
, the restriction
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T}k_{T},1)=\mathbf{Alg}(T)(f_{T},1)
forms a loose-monad module
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(f_{T},1)\colon\mathbf{Alg}(T)(1,1){\mathrel{\to}}E(j,T)
, whose collapse is the loose-cell
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T},1)
.
There is an isomorphism
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(f_{T},1)\cong E(j,u_{T})
of loose-monad modules, and hence an isomorphism
\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T},1)\cong\textup{«}\mathbf{Alg}(T)(j,u_{T})\textup{»}
between their collapses.
We can therefore take the collapse
\textup{«}\mathbf{Alg}(T)(j,u_{T})\textup{»}
for
\pi
by
Theorem

6.8
.

Density of
i_{T}
is immediate from density of
\pi
, which follows from
Lemma

6.7
.
For full faithfulness of
i_{T}
, it suffices by
[
AM24
, Corollary 3.28]
to exhibit an isomorphism
\mathbf{Opalg}(T)(1,1)\cong\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T},i_{T})
, which we do as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Opalg}(T)(1,1)=\textup{«}E(j,T)\textup{»}=\textup{«}E(j,u_{T})(1,i_{T}k_{T})\textup{»}=\textup{«}E(j,u_{T})\textup{»}(1,i_{T})\cong\mathbf{Alg}(T)(i_{T},i_{T})

∎

7.
The formal nerve theorem

The semanticiser theorem of
Theorem

6.8
may be viewed as a formulation of the nerve theorem that involves neither pullbacks nor presheaves. This makes it particularly general, as it holds even in settings in which neither exist. However, it is also a somewhat less convenient formulation, as semanticisers are an unfamiliar notion. In this section, we give sufficient conditions for semanticisers to exist in a virtual equipment, showing that it suffices for presheaf objects and pullbacks to exist. This provides the final step in our formal understanding of the nerve theorem, allowing us, in
Theorem

7.14
, to capture the algebra object for a relative monad as a pullback of the expected form.

7.1.
Presheaves in virtual equipments

We begin by defining the notion of a presheaf object in a virtual equipment, which classifies loose-cells into an object
A
by tight-cells into a corresponding object of presheaves
\mathcal{P}A
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
A
be an object of a strict virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
. A
presheaf object
for
A
comprises an object
\mathcal{P}A
and a dense loose-cell
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
, such that, for every loose-cell
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}A
, there is a unique tight-cell
\breve{p}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}A
satisfying
p=\pi_{A}(1,\breve{p})
.

(7.1.(1))

We denote by

\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}\mathrel{:=}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1558308}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle A(1,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1558308}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle A(1,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1267037}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle A(1,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1105389}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle A(1,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\colon A\to\mathcal{P}A

the
presheaf embedding
, which is the unique tight-cell satisfying
A(1,1)=\pi_{A}(1,\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A})
.

(7.1.(2))

For a tight-cell
j\colon A\to E
, we denote by

n_{j}\mathrel{:=}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1536981}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle E(j,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1536981}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle E(j,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1236289}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle E(j,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1073332}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle E(j,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\colon E\to\mathcal{P}A

the
nerve of
j
, which is the unique tight-cell satisfying
E(j,1)=\pi_{A}(1,n_{j})
. (By definition,
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
is the nerve of the identity
1_{A}
.)

(7.1.(3))

For
k\colon A\to B
a tight-cell between objects admitting presheaf objects, we denote by

k^{*}\mathrel{:=}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1852124}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\pi_{B}(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1852124}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\pi_{B}(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1529963}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\pi_{B}(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1386529}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{B}(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\colon\mathcal{P}B\to\mathcal{P}A

the
restriction
4
4

4

Here,
restriction
is used in a different sense than the restriction
p(f,g)
of a loose-cell along a pair of tight-cells, but there is no ambiguity between the two usages.
along
k
, which is the unique tight-cell satisfying
\pi_{B}(k,1)=\pi_{A}(1,k^{*})
.

Remark 7.2
.

Observe that
Definition

7.1
is essentially a double categorical (or, more precisely, an equipment-theoretic) notion of limit over a diagram with a single object
A
, where the projection
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
is required to be loose, and the unique mediating morphisms
\breve{p}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}A
are required to be tight: the difference in tightness between the projections and mediating morphisms is what permits the limit of a one-object diagram to be nontrivial. However,
Definition

7.1
is not a limit in the sense of
[
GP99
]
or
[
Pat24
]
, nor, as far as we are aware, in any other sense defined in the literature. This suggests it may be fruitful to study more general notions of double limit capturing this one. (Note that a general definition would be expected also to impose the two-dimensional universal property of
Proposition

7.5
, which is automatic under our assumptions.)

Remark 7.3
.

By virtue of
Lemma

7.8
, our definition of presheaf object is a strict analogue of the
Yoneda embeddings
of
[
Ker21
, Definition 1.1.2.2.1]
, and of the dual to the
Yoneda morphisms
of
[
Kou24
, Definition 4.2]
(the difference in variance is due to
[
Kou24
]
’s convention for the direction of distributors, which is opposite to ours).
[
Ker21
]
and
[
Kou24
]
do not require uniqueness of the mediating tight-cell
\breve{p}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}A
, though density of
\pi_{A}
implies that it is automatically essentially unique. Our (strict) presheaf objects are thus analogous to 2-categorical limits (a.k.a. 2-limits), whereas
[
Ker21
]
’s and
[
Kou24
]
’s (non-strict) presheaf objects are analogous to bicategorical limits (a.k.a. bilimits).

In a strict representable virtual equipment, our definition coincides with the
power objects
of
[
Lam22
, Definition 11.4]
assuming density of
\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
, which are motivated by the
power allegories
of
[
FS90
, §2.41]
. Similarly, by
Lemma

7.6
, presheaf objects are strongly related to the
power objects
of
[
MRW09
, §2.5]
and
strict representability
of tight-cells in the sense of
[
Par21
,
Par22
]
, though neither definition require density of
\pi_{A}
(equivalently of
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
). Related also are the
Yoneda situations
of
[
MT08
, Definition 6]
, though these do not classify loose-cells, but merely exhibit an embedding of tight-cells
X\to\mathcal{P}A
into loose-cells
X{\mathrel{\to}}A
(cf.
[
SW78
, §7(2)]
).

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
A
be an object admitting a presheaf object. For every pair of loose-cells
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}A
and
q\colon Y{\mathrel{\to}}A
, the canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}A(\breve{p},\breve{q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

exhibits
\mathcal{P}A(\breve{p},\breve{q})
as the right lift
q\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p
of
q
through
p
.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{P}A(\breve{p},\breve{q})\cong(\pi_{A}\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}\pi_{A})(\breve{p},\breve{q})\cong\pi_{A}(1,\breve{q})\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}\pi_{A}(1,\breve{p})=q\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p
using density of
\pi_{A}
,
[
AM24
, Lemma 3.6]
, and the universal property of the presheaf object.
∎

Consequently, while
Definition

7.1
appears only to specify a one-dimensional universal property, density of the projection
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
implies that presheaf objects further satisfy a two-dimensional universal property, analogous to the two-dimensional universal property of an algebra object (cf.
[
AM24
, Definition 6.33]
).

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
A
be an object admitting a presheaf object. Every 2-cell
\phi
of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

factors uniquely through a 2-cell
\breve{\phi}
, as below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}A(1,\breve{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}A(1,\breve{q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof.

Since
q\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p\cong\mathcal{P}A(\breve{p},\breve{q})
by
Lemma

7.4
, the factorisation follows directly from the universal property of the right lift
[
AM24
, Definition 3.1]
.
∎

Consequently, the universal property of a presheaf object may be rephrased in terms of a two-dimensional adjointness property.

Lemma 7.6
.

A dense loose-cell
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
exhibits a presheaf object for
A
if and only if, for each object
X\in\mathbb{X}
,

\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{582545}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle{({-})}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{582545}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle{({-})}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{468950}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle{({-})}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{407785}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle{({-})}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\colon\mathbb{X}\llbracket X,A\rrbracket\rightleftarrows\mathbb{X}[X,\mathcal{P}A]\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\pi_{A}(1,{-})

exhibits an isomorphism of categories.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
exhibits a presheaf object. That the assignment forms a bijection on objects is immediate from the universal property; that it forms a bijection on morphisms is immediate from
Proposition

7.5
.

Conversely, suppose that
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
exhibits an isomorphism of categories. The universal property of
Definition

7.1
follows trivially.
∎

The following lemma shows that presheaves interact nicely with restrictions.

Lemma 7.7
.

Consider a diagram
A\xrightarrow{f}B{\mathrel{\to}^{p}}C\xleftarrow{g}D
. Suppose that
A
and
B
admit presheaf objects. Then
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{1457454}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{1457454}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{1184050}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{1045067}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=(g\mathbin{;}\breve{p}\mathbin{;}f^{*})\colon D\to\mathcal{P}A
.

Proof.

We have

C(1,f^{*}\breve{p}g)=\mathcal{P}A(1,f^{*})(1,\breve{p}g)=\pi_{B}(f,1)(1,\breve{p}g)=\pi_{B}(f,\breve{p}g)=\pi_{B}(1,\breve{p})(f,g)=p(f,g)

hence
f^{*}\breve{p}g=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1457454}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1457454}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1184050}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1045067}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p(f,g)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
using the universal property of the presheaf object.
∎

We defer an in-depth exploration of the consequences of admitting a presheaf object. However, it is instructive to observe that it is possible to deduce from
Definition

7.1
a formal statement of the Yoneda lemma, as follows.

Lemma 7.8
.

For every object
A
admitting a presheaf object, there is an isomorphism
{\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},1)\cong\pi_{A}}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

7.4
,
\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},1)\cong\pi_{A}\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}A(1,1)\cong\pi_{A}
.
∎

In particular, for every tight-cell
\breve{p}\colon\cdot\to\mathcal{P}A
, which we may view as a presheaf on
A
, we have
\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},\breve{p})\cong\pi_{A}(1,\breve{p})=p
. It will also be useful, for
Section

9
, to observe that nerves are right adjoints relative to the presheaf embedding.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
j\colon A\to E
be a tight-cell for which
A
admits a presheaf object. Then
j\mathrel{\dashv_{\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}}}n_{j}
.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

7.8
, we have
E(j,1)=\pi_{A}(1,n_{j})\cong\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},n_{j})
.
∎

7.2.
Pullbacks in virtual equipments

Next, inevitably, we require a notion of pullback in a virtual equipment.
The appropriate notion of pullback in a 2-category is a pullback in the underlying 1-category, together with a universal property on 2-cells. A pullback in a virtual equipment is similar, except that we require the universal property on 2-cells to be suitably multiary.

Definition 7.10
.

A
pullback
of a cospan of 2-cells
\phi\colon p\Rightarrow r\Leftarrow q\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\psi
in a virtual double category is a commuting square of 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi\times_{r}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

such that, for each span of 2-cells

p\xLeftarrow{\chi_{1}}s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\xRightarrow{\chi_{2}}q\qquad(n\geq 0)

such that
\chi_{1}\mathbin{;}\phi=\chi_{2}\mathbin{;}\psi
, there is a unique
2-cell
\langle\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\rangle\colon s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow\phi\times_{r}\psi
rendering the following diagram commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi\times_{r}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\rangle}

Concisely, a pullback of 2-cells is a pullback in the corresponding hom-multicategory of
\mathbb{X}
. Pullbacks of 2-cells interact nicely with restrictions.

Lemma 7.11
.

In a virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
, the restriction of any pullback of 2-cells is also a pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\phi\times_{r}\psi)(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(x,y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(x,y)}

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{X}
admits loose-identities, 2-cells of the form

s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow t(x,y)
are in bijection with 2-cells of the form

X(1,x),s_{1},\dots,s_{n},Y(y,1)\Rightarrow t
, natural in
t
.
The result follows immediately by taking
t=(\phi\times_{r}\psi)
and using the universal property.
∎

Definition 7.12
.

A
pullback
of a cospan
f\colon A\to C\leftarrow B\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mug
of tight-cells in a virtual equipment
\mathbb{X}
is a pullback in the underlying
1-category of objects and tight-cells of
\mathbb{X}
, as on the left below, such that the induced square on the right below is a pullback of 2-cells.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\times_{C}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\times_{C}g)(1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(\pi_{2},\pi_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(\pi_{1},\pi_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(f\pi_{1},g\pi_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{\pi_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{\pi_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{g}}(\pi_{2},\pi_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbin{\smallfrown_{f}}(\pi_{1},\pi_{1})}

In particular, a pullback in
\mathbb{X}
is a 2-pullback in the tight 2-category
\underline{\mathbb{X}}
, but in general the pullback has a stronger universal property.

7.3.
The nerve theorem

In the presence of presheaf objects, we can give a construction of semanticisers in terms of pullbacks.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
n\colon E{\mathrel{\to}}A
be a loose-cell and let
k\colon A\to K
be a tight-cell for which
A
and
K
admit presheaf objects (
Definition

7.1
). Then the diagram on the left exhibits a semanticiser (
Definition

6.4
) if and only if the diagram on the right exhibits a pullback (
Definition

7.12
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n\times_{A}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{n\times_{A}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\kern 0.67557pt$\scriptstyle\hss\resizebox{399839}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\scriptstyle\hss\displaystyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\kern 0.67557pt$\scriptstyle\hss\resizebox{399839}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\scriptstyle\hss\textstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\kern 0.67557pt$\scriptstyle\hss\resizebox{264405}{87418}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\scriptstyle\hss\scriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\kern 0.67557pt$\scriptstyle\hss\resizebox{222565}{62441}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\scriptstyle\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}\limits}}

Proof.

This is a simple exercise in the use of
Lemma

7.7
. To show the two diagrams satisfy the same one-dimensional universal property, we need (1) their cones to be equivalent; (2) their mediating morphisms to be equivalent.

For (1), observe that a pair
(e,p)
of a tight-cell
e\colon\cdot\to E
and
p\colon\cdot{\mathrel{\to}}K
satisfying
n(1,e)=p(k,1)
is equivalent to a pair
(e,\breve{p})
of tight-cells
e\colon\cdot\to E
and
\breve{p}\colon\cdot\to\mathcal{P}K
satisfying
e\mathbin{;}\breve{n}=\breve{p}\mathbin{;}k^{*}
since we have
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1461764}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle n(1,e)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1461764}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle n(1,e)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1200840}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle n(1,e)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1063740}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle n(1,e)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=e\mathbin{;}\breve{n}
and
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1456179}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1456179}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1190620}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1052260}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p(k,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=\breve{p}\mathbin{;}k^{*}
.

For (2), observe that a tight-cell
\langle e,p\rangle\colon\cdot\to n\times_{A}k
satisfying
\langle e,p\rangle\mathbin{;}\pi_{1}=e
and
\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)=p
is equivalent to a tight-cell
\langle e,\breve{p}\rangle\colon\cdot\to n\times_{A}k
satisfying
\langle e,p\rangle\mathbin{;}\pi_{1}=e
and
\langle e,\breve{p}\rangle\mathbin{;}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{534062}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{534062}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{449796}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{422502}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=\breve{p}
since, by
Lemma

7.7
, we have
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{2498332}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{2498332}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{2134069}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1962627}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}(1,\langle e,p\rangle)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=\langle e,p\rangle\mathbin{;}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{534062}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{534062}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{449796}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{422502}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
.

To show the two diagrams satisfy the same two-dimensional universal property involves similar reasoning involving (1) equivalence of cones; and (2) equivalence of mediating 2-cells. For (1), observe that the universal property of the semanticiser involves a pair of 2-cells of the following form for each pair
e,e^{\prime}\colon\cdot\to E
of tight-cells and each pair
p,p^{\prime}\colon\cdot{\mathrel{\to}}K
of loose-cells,

\displaystyle\chi_{1}

\displaystyle\colon E(1,e),s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(1,e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\chi_{2}

\displaystyle\colon p,s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow p^{\prime}

whereas the universal property of the pullback involves a pair of 2-cells of the following form.

\displaystyle\chi_{1}^{\prime}\colon s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(\pi_{1},\pi_{1})

\displaystyle\chi_{2}^{\prime}\colon s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}K(\pi_{2},\pi_{2})

However, by
Lemma

7.11
, restricting the pullback of the cospan
E(\pi_{1},\pi_{1})\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}A(\breve{n}\pi_{1},k^{*}\pi_{2})\Leftarrow\mathcal{P}K(\pi_{2},\pi_{2})
along
\langle e,\breve{p}\rangle
and
\langle e^{\prime},\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{357944}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{335725}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\rangle
exhibits a pullback of the cospan
E(e,e^{\prime})\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}A(\breve{n}e,k^{*}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{357944}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{335725}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})\Leftarrow\mathcal{P}K(\breve{p},\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{357944}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{335725}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})
, which satisfies a universal property involving a pair of 2-cells of the following form.

\displaystyle\chi_{1}^{\prime\prime}\colon s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow E(e,e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\chi_{2}^{\prime\prime}\colon s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}K(\breve{p},\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{357944}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{335725}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})

By transposing
e
and
\breve{p}
, and using the universal property of
\mathcal{P}K
, the 2-cells
\chi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\chi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are equivalent to the 2-cells
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
. Conversely, taking
\langle e,\breve{p}\rangle
and
\langle e^{\prime},\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{410875}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{357944}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{335725}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p^{\prime}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}\rangle
to be identities in
\chi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\chi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
recovers
\chi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\chi_{2}^{\prime}
. (2) then follows by completely analogous reasoning.
∎

Theorem 7.14

(Nerve theorem)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an exact virtual equipment, let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense tight-cell, and let
T
be a
j
-monad. Suppose that
A
and
\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
admit presheaf objects. Then
u\colon D\to E
exhibits an algebra object for
T
if and only if there is a loose-cell
\breve{p}\colon D\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Opalg}(T))
exhibiting the following diagram as a pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Opalg}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}

In this case,
\breve{p}\cong n_{i_{T}}
, exhibiting the comparison tight-cell
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Opalg}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
as dense and fully faithful.

Proof.

Direct from
Theorems

6.8
,
7.13
and
6.10
.
∎

Remark 7.15
.

From
Theorem

7.14
, taking
j=1
, we essentially recover
[
Str74
,
SW78
]
, which establish analogous theorems in the context of 2-categories endowed with attributes and Yoneda structures respectively (there is a strong relationship between Yoneda structures and presheaf objects in virtual equipments, as established in
[
Kou24
, Theorem 4.24]
).

The following establishes that every suitably complete virtual equipment admits algebra objects for relative monads with small domain. In the case of enriched categories, it is weaker than the existence theorem of
[
AM24
, Corollary 8.20]
(which requires neither smallness nor density), but the assumptions are often convenient to verify in practice.

Corollary 7.16
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an exact strict virtual equipment and let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense tight-cell. If
A
admits a presheaf object, and
\mathbb{X}
admits pullbacks along
n_{j}\colon E\to\mathcal{P}A
, then a
j
-monad admits an algebra object if its opalgebra object admits a presheaf object.

Proof.

Direct from
Theorem

7.14
.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

Theorem

7.14
involves several concepts that look like colimit notions (opalgebra objects and collapses) and several concepts that look like limit notions (algebra objects, semanticisers, pullbacks, and presheaf objects). In this light,
Theorems

6.8
and
7.14
correspond to a notion of exactness, describing an interaction between certain limits and colimits, while
Lemmas

7.13
and
9.3
corresponds to the construction of one kind of limit from another, akin to the construction of arbitrary limits from products and equalisers. Limits and colimits for virtual double categories have not yet been defined. However, even in the representable case, these concepts do not appear to be double limits and colimits in the classical sense (cf.
Remark

7.2
), since they make fundamental use of restrictions. It remains to be seen whether there are natural notions of limit and colimit that capture these concepts.

8.
The enriched nerve theorem

We have now developed the abstract theory necessary to establish the nerve theorem for enriched relative monads. In particular, we shall show that
Theorem

7.14
may be applied in the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
of categories enriched in a monoidal category
{\mathbb{V}}
(
[
AM24
, Definition 8.1]
), thereby obtaining the desired nerve theorem. We make no assumptions on the monoidal category
{\mathbb{V}}
except where otherwise specified.

First, we must verify the assumptions of
Theorem

7.14
on the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
. Recall that a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor
p\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
comprises an object
p(x,y)\in{\mathbb{V}}
for each
x,y\in|A|
, along with pre- and postcomposition actions. A
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monad thus comprises

(8.0.(1))

a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor
T\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
;

(8.0.(2))

a
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation
\circ\colon T,T\Rightarrow T
;

(8.0.(3))

a
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation
\mathsf{I}\colon{}\Rightarrow T
,

satisfying associativity and unitality laws.
Unwrapping the definition, we see that each loose-monad comprises, for each pair of objects
x,y\in|A|
, an object
T(x,y)\in{\mathbb{V}}
; for each heteromorphism
f\in T(x,y)
and heteromorphism
g\in T(y,z)
, a heteromorphism
(g\circ f)\in T(x,z)
; and for each morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
A
, a heteromorphism
\mathsf{I}_{f}\in T(x,y)
, subject to unitality and associativity axioms.
This structure strongly resembles that of a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category. In fact, every
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monad defines a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
\textup{«}p\textup{»}
, together with an identity-on-objects
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor
A\to\textup{«}p\textup{»}
.
5
5

5

The converse is also true: one may show that
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monads on
A
are in bijection with identity-on-objects
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors from
A
(cf.
[
Luc16
,
SSR17
]
). However, we defer a general proof to future work, as we have no need for it here.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
T
be a
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monad on a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
A
. The
collapse
of
T
is the category
\textup{«}T\textup{»}
defined by
|\textup{«}T\textup{»}|\mathrel{:=}|A|
and
\textup{«}T\textup{»}(x,y)\mathrel{:=}T(x,y)
. Denote by
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T}\colon A\to\textup{«}T\textup{»}
the identity-on-objects
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor defined by the unit of
T
.

As the terminology suggests, the collapse of a
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monad exhibits a collapse in the sense of
Definition

5.2
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be a monoidal category. The virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
is exact.

Proof.

First observe that, for every
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched loose-monad
T
, the identity natural transformation on its underlying
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor exhibits a cartesian 2-cell, since
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T}
is identity-on-objects and the hom-objects of
\textup{«}T\textup{»}
are defined by
T
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}T\textup{»}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}T\textup{»}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\scriptstyle\pi$}}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\scriptstyle\pi$}}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

Note that
\textup{«}A(1,1)\textup{»}=A(1,1)
by definition. Consider a loose-monad morphism
(f,\phi)\colon T\to B(1,1)
, i.e. a
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation

\phi_{x,y}\colon T(x,y)\Rightarrow B(|f|x,|f|y)

compatible with the multiplication and unit of
T
. The full image factorisation of
f
into an identity-on-objects
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor followed by a fully faithful
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor defines a unique factorisation of
(f,\phi)
.

Now consider a loose-monad module
p\colon T^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}}T
. We define a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor
\textup{«}p\textup{»}\colon\textup{«}T^{\prime}\textup{»}{\mathrel{\to}}\textup{«}T\textup{»}
by
\textup{«}p\textup{»}(x,y)\mathrel{:=}p(x,y)
. The compatibility laws for
p
correspond exactly to the compatibility laws for
\textup{«}p\textup{»}
. Since
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}}
and
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T}
are identity-on-objects, the identity natural transformation exhibits a cartesian 2-cell.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}T\textup{»}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{«}T^{\prime}\textup{»}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\scriptstyle\pi$}}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\scriptstyle\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{«}p\textup{»}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

Note that the collapse of the identity loose-monad module corresponding to a loose-monad
T
is precisely the collapse of
T
. Consider a loose-monad transformation
\psi
as in
Definition

5.2
, which is a
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation
p_{1}(x_{0},x_{1}),\ldots,p_{n}(x_{n-1},x_{n})\Rightarrow q(|f|x_{0},|f^{\prime}|x_{n})
satisfying compatibility laws. It is clear that
\psi
defines a
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation framed by the full images as previously described, factoring
\psi
. Finally, consider a loose-monad module
p\colon\textup{«}T\textup{»}{\mathrel{\to}}\textup{«}T^{\prime}\textup{»}
between collapses. Since the coprojections
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T}
and
\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}}
are identity-on-objects, we trivially have
\textup{«}p(\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T},\text{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-180.0}{$\pi$}}_{T^{\prime}})\textup{»}=p
.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

Loose-monads have also been called
promonads
or
profunctor monads
, in accordance with the terminology
profunctors
for what we call distributors. In theoretical computer science, they appear as the
arrows
of
[
Hug00
, §4]
(cf.
[
HJ06
, Definition 4.1]
).

Modulo strength, loose-monad morphisms from
T
to
A(1,1)
are the
algebras for an arrow
of
[
JH06
,
JHH09
]
. The collapse appears as the
Freyd category
associated to the arrow
T
(cf.
[
HJ06
, Theorem 5.4]
), and the one-dimensional universal property of a collapse is consequently established for the equipment
{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}
in
[
JH06
,
JHH09
]
.

Corollary 8.4

(
[
AM24
, Theorem 8.21]
)
.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be a monoidal category. Every relative monad in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
admits an opalgebra object.

Proof.

By
Proposition

8.2
,
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
is exact, so admits opalgebra objects by
Theorem

6.2
.
∎

Next, we verify that the notion of presheaf object in
Definition

7.1
does indeed capture the notion of presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category (
[
AM24
, Definition 8.5]
).

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be a monoidal category and let
A
be a small
{\mathbb{V}}
-category admitting a presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category. The presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
\mathcal{P}A
is a presheaf object in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, for which the Yoneda embedding
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{P}A
exhibits a presheaf embedding.

Proof.

We define a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor
\pi_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}A{\mathrel{\to}}A
assigning
(a,p)\mapsto p(a)
, with left-action

\{\circ^{p}_{a^{\prime},a}\colon A(a^{\prime},a)\otimes p(a)\to p(a^{\prime})\}_{a^{\prime}\in|A|,a\in|A|,p\in|\mathcal{P}A|}

given by the left-action of the presheaf
p
, and right-action given by the universal
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation associated to the object of
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformations from
p
to
p^{\prime}
.

\{\varpi^{p,p^{\prime}}_{a}\colon p(a)\otimes\mathcal{P}A(p,p^{\prime})\to p^{\prime}(a)\}_{a\in|A|,p\in|\mathcal{P}A|,p^{\prime}\in|\mathcal{P}A|}

The laws for a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor follow from the laws for a presheaf, together with
{\mathbb{V}}
-naturality. Density of
\pi_{A}
follows from
[
AM24
, Lemma 8.7]
.

Let
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}A
be a
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor. We aim to define a
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor
\breve{p}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}A
. Observe that, in order that
\pi_{A}
exhibit a presheaf object, for each
x\in|X|
and
a\in|A|
, we must have
(\pi_{A}(1,\breve{p}))(a,x)=\pi_{A}(a,|\breve{p}|(x))=|\breve{p}|(x)(a)
, with the left-action of
|\breve{p}|(x)
given by the left-action of
p
at
x
. This uniquely determines the action of
\breve{p}
on objects. Indeed, this is a valid assignment, as the laws for the
{\mathbb{V}}
-presheaf then follow from the laws for the
{\mathbb{V}}
-distributor. For the action of
\breve{p}
on hom-objects, observe that, for each pair of objects
x,x^{\prime}\in|X|
, we have
\mathcal{P}A(|\breve{p}|(x),|\breve{p}|(x^{\prime}))=\mathcal{P}A(p({-},x),p({-},x^{\prime}))=(p\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p)(x,x^{\prime})
by
[
AM24
, Lemma 8.7]
. The right-action of
\pi_{A}
thus uniquely determines the choice of the morphism
\breve{p}_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon X(x,x^{\prime})\to\mathcal{P}A(|\breve{p}|(x),|\breve{p}|(x^{\prime}))
in
{\mathbb{V}}
to be that corresponding to the canonical
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation
X(1,1)\Rightarrow p\mathbin{\blacktriangleleft}p
.

That the Yoneda embedding exhibits a presheaf embedding is precisely the Yoneda lemma
[
AM24
, Example 8.6]
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

While presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories may exist even on
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories that are not small, existence of presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories is generally a strong size constraint. For instance, when
{\mathbb{V}}={\mathbf{Set}}
, a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category admits a presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category (i.e. a locally small presheaf category) if and only it is essentially small
[
Fol79
,
FS95
]
. We do not know whether there exists an example of a non-thin monoidal category
{\mathbb{V}}
together with a
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
A
that admits a presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
\mathcal{P}A
but that is not
Morita-small
, i.e. for which there does not exist a small
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
A^{\prime}
for which
\mathcal{P}A\simeq\mathcal{P}(A^{\prime})
.

Finally, we observe that the usual construction of pullbacks of
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors produces pullbacks in the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be a monoidal category admitting pullbacks. Then the virtual equipment
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
admits pullbacks.

Proof.

Given a cospan of
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors
f\colon A\to C\leftarrow B\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mug
, the apex of the pullback is the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category
f\times_{C}g
, whose objects are pairs
(a\in|A|,b\in|B|)
such that
|f|a=|g|b
, and whose hom-objects are given by pullbacks in
{\mathbb{V}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\times_{C}g)((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(a,a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(|f|a,|g|b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{b,b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{a,a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Composition and identities in
f\times_{C}g
are uniquely determined by the fact that the projections form a span of
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors
A\xleftarrow{\pi_{1}}f\times_{C}g\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}C
: this is the pullback in the 1-category of
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories and
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors.
The actions of the
{\mathbb{V}}
-functors form a pullback of 2-cells in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
, since every
{\mathbb{V}}
-natural transformation
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\Rightarrow(A\times_{C}B)(1,1)
is, in particular, a family of morphisms to which we may apply the universal property of pullbacks in
{\mathbb{V}}
.
∎

The following is now immediate.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be a complete left- and right-closed monoidal category and let
j\colon A\to E
be a dense
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor with small domain. For a
j
-monad
T
, the
{\mathbb{V}}
-category of
T
-algebras may be constructed as the following pullback in
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Kl}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Above, we denote by
\mathcal{P}
the
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched presheaf construction; and by
n_{j}\colon E\to\mathcal{P}A
the nerve of
j
, which sends an object
e\in|E|
to the restricted Yoneda embedding
E(j{-},e)
.

Furthermore, the unlabelled
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor is
{\mathbb{V}}
-naturally isomorphic to the nerve of the comparison
{\mathbb{V}}
-functor
i_{T}\colon\mathbf{Kl}(T)\to\mathbf{Alg}(T)
, exhibiting it as dense and fully faithful.

Proof.

Follows directly from
Theorem

7.14
, using that
{{\mathbb{V}}\textup{-}{\mathbf{\mathbb{C}at}}}
is exact (
Proposition

8.2
) and, under the given assumptions, admits presheaf objects for small
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories (
[
AM24
, §8.1]
and
Proposition

8.5
) and pullbacks (
Proposition

8.7
).
∎

Remark 8.9
.

When
A
is large, presheaf
{\mathbb{V}}
-categories can no longer be assumed to exist, and so
Theorem

7.14
may not be applied. In this case,
Theorem

6.8
may be used directly.

Remark 8.10
.

If an algebra object for
T
is already known to exist, the relative monadicity theorem
[
AM25
]
facilitates an alternative proof of
Theorem

7.14
, as was sketched in
[
AM25
, Example 5.11]
. However, we find this alternative proof strategy to be conceptually dissatisfying, as the assumption that the algebra object exists obscures the fact that the existence of the pullback is both necessary and sufficient for the existence of the algebra object.

It follows from
[
AM24
, Corollary 8.20]
that, when
{\mathbb{V}}
is complete and closed,
{\mathbb{V}}
-enriched relative monads with small domains admit algebra objects.
Theorem

8.8
thus establishes the same result under almost the same conditions, except with the additional assumption that the root be dense (however, the conclusion is also stronger, establishing that
i_{T}
is dense).

9.
Loose-monads and
-relative monads

We conclude by observing that presheaf objects permit loose-monads to be captured by relative monads. In doing so, we give another application of the semanticiser theorem, in characterising the algebras for
-relative monads.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a strict virtual equipment, and let
A
be an object admitting a presheaf object. There is an isomorphism

\mathbf{RMnd}(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A})\cong\textup{\sagittarius}\mathbf{Mnd}(A)

between the category of
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
-monads and the category of loose-monads on
A
.

Proof.

By
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.22]
, we have the following pullback of categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{RMnd}(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{\sagittarius}\mathbf{Mnd}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}[A,\mathcal{P}A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}\llbracket A,A\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

However, by
Lemma

7.6
, the bottom functor is an isomorphism, hence so too is the top functor.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

From
Theorem

9.1
, we recover
[
ACU10
,
ACU15
]
regarding the correspondence between arrows (
Remark

8.3
) and monads relative to the Yoneda embedding.

Given that
-relative monads correspond to loose-monads, we should hope that it is possible also to characterise their (op)algebra objects in terms of the loose-monads. In an exact equipment, this is indeed possible. First, we observe the following relationship between semanticisers and presheaf objects.

Lemma 9.3
.

The following square exhibits a semanticiser if and only if
\pi\colon P{\mathrel{\to}}B
exhibits a presheaf object and
\breve{r}\colon P\to\mathcal{P}A
exhibits the restriction of
f
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Proof.

Since
\pi_{A}
is dense, it suffices by
Lemma

6.7
to show that the one-dimensional universal property of the semanticiser is equivalent to the one-dimensional universal property of the presheaf object.

The universal property of the presheaf object asks that, for each loose-cell
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}B
, there is a unique tight-cell
\breve{p}\colon X\to P
such that
p=\pi(1,\breve{p})
.

The universal property of the semanticiser asks that, for each tight-cell
e\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}\mathcal{P}A
and loose-cell
p\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}B
such that
p(f,1)=\pi_{A}(1,e)
, there is a unique tight-cell
\langle e,p\rangle\colon X\to P
such that
\langle e,p\rangle\mathbin{;}\breve{r}=e
and
\pi(1,\langle e,p\rangle)=p
. Note that the cone condition uniquely determines
e=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1450719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1450719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1175330}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1030415}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
.

Thus, supposing that
\pi
exhibits a presheaf object and that
\breve{r}=f^{*}
, it follows from
Lemma

7.7
that
\breve{p}\mathbin{;}f^{*}=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1450719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1450719}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1175330}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1030415}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle p(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
, so that the square exhibits a semanticiser. Conversely, supposing the square exhibits a semanticiser,
\pi
trivially exhibits a presheaf object. Furthermore, commutativity of the square expresses that
r=\pi_{A}(1,\breve{r})=\pi(f,1)
and hence that
\breve{r}=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1485796}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\pi(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1485796}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\pi(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1204374}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\pi(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{1054384}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\pi(f,1)\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}=f^{*}
.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an exact virtual equipment, let
A
be an object admitting a presheaf object, and let
T
be a
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
-monad. The opalgebra object for
T
is given by the collapse of the corresponding loose-monad. Furthermore,
T
admits an algebra object if and only if
\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
admits a presheaf object, in which case they are isomorphic over
\mathcal{P}A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Opalg}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T}}

Proof.

By
Theorem

9.1
, the loose-monad associated to
T
is given by
\mathcal{P}A(\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A},T)
; by
Theorem

6.2
, its collapse exhibits the opalgebra object for
T
. The second statement then follows from
Theorem

6.8
, using that
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
is dense by
Lemma

7.8
, together with
Lemma

9.3
with respect to squares of the following form.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Opalg}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

∎

It follows from
Theorems

9.1
and
4.11
that every
j
-monad
T
induces an associated
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
-monad
(T\mathbin{;}n_{j})
by postcomposing
n_{j}\colon E\to\mathcal{P}A
; alternatively, this can be seen to follow from
[
AM24
, Proposition 5.37]
using
Lemma

7.9
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We consequently recover the following generalisation of
Corollary

9.4
(cf.
[
Ark22
, Remark 5.5.7]
). The relevance of this characterisation to the nerve theorem will be expounded elsewhere.

Corollary 9.5
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an exact virtual equipment and let
j\colon A\to E
be a tight-cell. Suppose that
A
admits a presheaf object. The
\!\text{\char 136\relax}\!_{A}
-monad
(T\mathbin{;}n_{j})
admits an algebra object if and only if
\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
admits a presheaf object, in which case they are isomorphic over
\mathcal{P}A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Alg}(T\mathbin{;}n_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Opalg}(T))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{T}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{T\mathbin{;}n_{j}}}

Proof.

Immediate from
Corollary

9.4
, observing that
\mathbf{Opalg}(T\mathbin{;}n_{j})\cong\mathbf{Opalg}(T)
under
A
by
[
AM24
, Remark 6.28]
.
∎

Appendix A
Strictification for virtual equipments

Recall that virtual double categories, their functors, and tight transformations form a 2-category
{\mathbf{VDbl}}

[
CS10
, Definition 3.1]
. An
equivalence of virtual double categories
is an equivalence in
{\mathbf{VDbl}}
.

Theorem A.1
.

For every virtual double category
\mathbb{X}
admitting restrictions, there is an equivalent virtual double category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, with the same underlying category of tight-cells, admitting a strictly functorial choice of restrictions.

Proof.

We define a virtual double category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
as follows.

(A.1.(1))

The category of objects and tight-cells is given by that of
\mathbb{X}
.

(A.1.(2))

A loose-cell from
A
to
D
is a diagram
A\xrightarrow{g}B{\mathrel{\to}^{p}}C\xleftarrow{f}D
in
\mathbb{X}
.

(A.1.(3))

A 2-cell with frame

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1},p_{1},g_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{2},p_{2},g_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{n-1},p_{n-1},g_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{n},p_{n},g_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime},p^{\prime},g^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is a 2-cell with the following frame in
\mathbb{X}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}(f_{1},g_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}(f_{2},g_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n-1}(f_{n-1},g_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n}(f_{n},g_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(A.1.(4))

Composition of 2-cells is given by that in
\mathbb{X}
.

(A.1.(5))

The identity of a loose-cell is given by that in
\mathbb{X}
.

The associativity and unitality laws for
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
follow from those for
\mathbb{X}
. There is a canonical functor
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
sending each loose-cell
(f,p,g)
to
p(f,g)
, which is the identity on the tight category and fully faithful on 2-cells. It is trivially surjective on loose-cells, since each loose-cell
p\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}D
is the image of the diagram
A=\!=A{\mathrel{\to}^{p}}D=\!=D
, and thus the canonical functor is an equivalence of virtual double categories.

It remains to show that
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
admits a strict choice of restrictions. To do so, observe that the cartesian 2-cell in
\mathbb{X}
on the left below exhibits a cartesian 2-cell in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
on the right below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(f,g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(f^{\prime}f,g^{\prime}g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,p,g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ff^{\prime},p,gg^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cart}}

It is clear that this choice of restrictions is strictly functorial, being inherited from composition of tight-cells in
\mathbb{X}
.
∎

One might expect the definition of a strict virtual equipment to include also a condition on the loose-identities, i.e. that composition of loose-cells is strictly unital. However, as the following lemma shows, we may always choose composites in a given virtual double category in such a way as to make this hold.

Lemma A.2
.

Every opcartesian 2-cell,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{opcart}}

for which the object
A_{i}
admits a loose-identity (for some fixed
0\leq i\leq n
), factors through an opcartesian 2-cell as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(1,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{opcart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{opcart}}

Proof.

That the given opcartesian 2-cell factors through
some
2-cell follows immediately from the universal property of the opcartesian 2-cell defining
A(1,1)
. That the 2-cell is opcartesian follows from opcartesianness of the given 2-cell.
∎

We conclude by observing that
Theorem

A.1
gives a full strictification result for representable virtual equipments.

Corollary A.3
.

For every pseudo double category admitting companions and conjoints, there is an equivalent strict double category admitting a strictly functorial choice of companions and conjoints.

Proof.

To every pseudo double category
\mathbb{X}
, there is an equivalent strict double category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}

[
GP99
, Theorem 7.5]
. Applying
Theorem

A.1
then produces an equivalent pseudo double category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}
admitting a strictly functorial choice of restrictions. By
[
Shu08
, Theorem 4.1]
, a (strictly functorial) choice of restrictions in a pseudo double category is equivalent to a (strictly functorial) choice of companions and conjoints. It remains to show that
\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}
is actually a strict double category. The loose-composite of
(f,p,g)
and
(f^{\prime},p^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}
is represented by the loose-composite of
p(f,g)
and
p^{\prime}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, and so if loose-composition is strict in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, it is also strict in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Remark A.4
.

Michael Shulman has suggested an alternative approach to the strictification of pseudo double categories with companions and conjoints
[
Shu22
]
, in which one modifies the tight category – equipping each tight-cell with a specified companion and conjoint – rather than modifying the loose bicategory. However, such a strictification
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the original pseudo double category
\mathbb{X}
only in a weak sense, in which the tight 2-categories
\underline{\mathbb{X}}
and
\underline{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}
are merely biequivalent rather than being 2-equivalent, so that
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
are not equivalent as objects of
{\mathbf{VDbl}}
.
